for the multiparty session types in
Example

2.3.2
.

\lrcorner

The subtyping relation we presented induces a substitution
principle.

Proposition 3.1.1

(
Safe
substitution principle)
.

If
S\leqslant_{*}T
, then a process that uses an endpoint according
to
S
can be
safely
substituted with a process that
uses the endpoint according to
T
.

Note that we highlight the word
safe
as we want to
point out that the subtyping relation under analysis is
studied to preserve the safety of the communication.
We will give more details in
Section

3.1.1
.

Remark 3.1.1
.

The reader might be confused by the formulation of
Proposition

3.1.1

as it seems to treat the substitution in the wrong direction
(left-to-right). However, it is important to think about the
subject of the principle,
i.e.
processes in
Proposition

3.1.1
.
Indeed, the same principle
can be formulated in a right-to-left fashion as in
Liskov and Wing 1994

by taking into account the endpoints and not the processes.
The two formulations turn out to be equivalent (see
Gay 2016
for
more details).

\lrcorner

3.1.1
Unfair Subtyping

In
Section

3.1
we pointed out the
safety
-preserving
property of
Proposition

3.1.1
.
Indeed, the two substitutions proposed in
Example

3.1.1

do not break the correctness of the communication between the entities.
However, the subtyping relation introduced by
Gay and Hole 2005

can break the
liveness
of the session on which it is applied.

Example 3.1.2
.

Consider the session types
S_{b}
and
S_{s}
from
Example

2.3.1
.
The following judgment can be derived

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}S_{b}&=&{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}+{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}&\leqslant_{*}&S_{b}^{\infty}&=&{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}^{\infty}\end{array}

Let us look at the infinite derivation tree

\displaystyle\penalty\vdots

[us-tag-out]

\displaystyle S_{b}\leqslant_{*}S_{b}^{\infty}

[us-tag-out]

\displaystyle S_{b}\leqslant_{*}S_{b}^{\infty}

[us-tag-out]

\displaystyle{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}+{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{*}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}^{\infty}

As a consequence,
buyer
can be replaced with

buyer
∞
behaving as
S_{b}^{\infty}

by using
Proposition

3.1.1
(it only
add
s items).
No matter how it reduces,
the
buyer
∞
will never
pay
the amount and the
session will never terminate. Moreover, the only run of the
session is infinite and
fair
since it does not contain
any weakly terminating state.
This example applies to
Example

5.1.1
as well.

\lrcorner

Intuitively, the problem of the subtyping we investigated so far lies
in the contravariance of the output rule
[us-tag-out]
.
When such rule is applied, some branches are cut and it might happen
that all those branches leading to termination are removed.
This is what happens in
Example

3.1.2
.
Hence, the subtyping of
Gay and Hole 2005
must be refined
to preserve the termination property of the session.

3.2
Fair Subtyping

Fair subtyping is a
liveness
-preserving variant
of the subtyping proposed by
Gay and Hole 2005
.
In this section we present such relation as a purely
coinductive predicate. However, in
Section

3.2.3

we will show an equivalent and more involved formulation
obtained by equipping the rules in
Figure

3.1
with
a
corule
(see
Section

1.2
). While the generalized
inference system is intriguing since it points out the safety
and liveness aspects of the predicate, the formulation we give in
this section significantly simplifies the correctness proofs and
it will be easily integrated in the type systems in
Part

II
.
Since binary sessions are the simplest multiparty ones, we refer to the binary
case when it allows to simplify the presentation of some features of the property
(
e.g.
examples, description of the rules) while we present all the results
in the more general multiparty one.

\frac{\mathstrut}{\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{n}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}}\quad\textsc{fsb-end}\hypertarget{rule:fsb-end}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}\quad\forall i\in I:n_{i}\leq n}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{n}\sum_{i\in I\cup J}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\quad\textsc{fsb-tag-in}\hypertarget{rule:fsb-tag-in}{}\\
\frac{S\leqslant_{n}T}{\pi U.S\leqslant_{n}\pi U.T}\quad\textsc{fsb-channel}\hypertarget{rule:fsb-channel}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}\quad\forall i\in I:n_{i}\leq n}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{n}\sum_{i\in I}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\quad\textsc{fsb-tag-out-1}\hypertarget{rule:fsb-tag-out-1}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}\quad\exists i\in I:n_{i}<n}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I\cup J}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{n}\sum_{i\in I}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\quad\textsc{fsb-tag-out-2}\hypertarget{rule:fsb-tag-out-2}{}

Figure 3.2:
Inference system for fair subtyping - Binary

\frac{\mathstrut}{\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{n}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}}\quad\textsc{fsm-end}\hypertarget{rule:fsm-end}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}\quad\forall i\in I:n_{i}\leq n}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{n}\sum_{i\in I\cup J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\quad\textsc{fsm-tag-in}\hypertarget{rule:fsm-tag-in}{}\and\frac{S\leqslant_{n}T}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi U.S\leqslant_{n}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi U.T}\quad\textsc{fsm-channel}\hypertarget{rule:fsm-channel}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}\quad\forall i\in I:n_{i}\leq n}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{n}\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\quad\textsc{fsm-tag-out-1}\hypertarget{rule:fsm-tag-out-1}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}\quad\exists i\in I:n_{i}<n}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I\cup J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{n}\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\quad\textsc{fsm-tag-out-2}\hypertarget{rule:fsm-tag-out-2}{}

Figure 3.3:
Inference system for fair subtyping - Multiparty

Fair subtyping for binary session types is defined as the
relation
\leqslant_{n}
coinductively defined by the inference system
in
Figure

3.2
, where
n
ranges over natural numbers. The characterization
of fair subtyping that we consider is the relation
{\leqslant}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\leqslant_{n}}
.
Fair subtyping for multiparty session types is analogously defined
(see
Figure

3.3
).
The rules for deriving
S\leqslant_{n}T
are quite similar to those of the standard
subtyping relation for session types
[
Gay and Hole 2005
]
:
[fsb-end]
states
reflexivity of subtyping on terminated session types;
[fsb-channel]

relates higher-order session types with the same polarity and payload type;

[fsb-tag-in]
is the usual covariant rule for the input of tags (the set
of tags in the larger session type includes those in the smaller one);

[fsb-tag-out-2]
is the usual contravariant rule for the output of tags
(the set of tags in the smaller session type includes those in the larger one).
Overall, these rules entail a “simulation” between the behaviors described by

S_{b}
and
S_{b}^{\prime}
whereby all inputs offered by
S_{b}
are also offered by
S_{b}^{\prime}
and all
outputs performed by
S_{b}^{\prime}
are also performed by
S_{b}
.
The main differences between
\leqslant
and the subtyping relation of
Gay and Hole 2005

are the presence of an invariant rule for outputs

[fsb-tag-out-1]
and the natural number
n
annotating each subtyping
judgment
S\leqslant_{n}T
. Intuitively, this number estimates how much
S_{b}
and
S_{b}^{\prime}

differ in terms of performed outputs. In all rules but
[fsb-tag-out-2]
,
the annotation in the conclusion of the rule is just an upper bound of the
annotations found in the premises. In
[fsb-tag-out-2]
, where the sets of
output tags in related session types may differ, the annotation
n
is required
to be a
strict
upper bound for at least one of the premises. That is,
there must be at least one premise in which the annotation strictly decreases,
while no restriction is imposed on the others. Intuitively, this ensures the
existence of a tag shared by the two related session types whose corresponding
continuations are slightly less different. So, the annotation
n
provides an
upper bound to the number of applications of
[fsb-tag-out-2]
along any
path (
i.e.
any sequence of actions) shared by
S_{b}
and
S_{b}^{\prime}
that leads to
termination. In the particular case when
n=0
, the rule
[fsb-tag-out-2]

cannot be applied, so that
S_{b}^{\prime}
may perform all the outputs also performed by

S_{b}
.

Remark 3.2.1

(Invariant delegation)
.

As it can be noted from
[fsb-channel]
and
[fsm-channel]
,
we require that the type of the channel being exchanged in the supertype
matches that in the subtype. We made such a decision
since we found out that allowing co/contravariance of input/output of channels
may break the liveness of the session.
We will give more details in
Section

4.2.4
.

\lrcorner

Fair subtyping allows us to reject the subtyping instance in
Example

3.1.2

that breaks the liveness of the involved session.

Example 3.2.1
.

Consider the session types

\begin{array}[]{rclrcl}S_{b}&=&{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}+{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}&S_{b}^{\prime}&=&{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}^{\prime}+{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\end{array}

from
Examples

2.3.1
and
3.1.1

describing the
buyer
purchasing an arbitrary number of items
and the behavior of the
buyer
always purchasing an even
number of items, respectively. Consider also

S_{b}^{\infty}={!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}^{\infty}

from
Example

3.1.2
,
which describes the behavior of a
buyer
attempting to
add
an
infinite number of items without ever
pay
ing the
seller
.
We have
S_{b}\leqslant S_{b}^{\prime}
and
S_{b}\not\leqslant S_{b}^{\infty}
. Indeed, we can derive

\displaystyle\penalty\mathstrut\smash{\vdots}

\displaystyle S_{b}\leqslant_{1}S_{b}^{\prime}

[fsb-tag-out-2]

\displaystyle S_{b}\leqslant_{2}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S_{b}^{\prime}

[fsb-end]

\displaystyle{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{0}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}

[fsb-tag-out-2]

\displaystyle S_{b}\leqslant_{1}S_{b}^{\prime}

but there is no derivation for
S_{b}\leqslant_{n}S_{b}^{\infty}
no matter how large
n
is
chosen.
Note that there are infinitely many sequences of actions of
S_{b}
that cannot be
performed by both
S_{b}^{\prime}
and
S_{b}^{\infty}
. In particular,
S_{b}^{\prime}
cannot perform any sequence
of actions consisting of an odd number of
add
outputs followed by a

pay
output, whereas
S_{b}^{\infty}
cannot perform any sequence of
add

outputs followed by a
pay
output. Nonetheless, there is a path shared
by
S_{b}
and
S_{b}^{\prime}
that leads into a region of
S_{b}
and
S_{b}^{\prime}
in which no more
differences are detectable. The annotations in the derivation tree measures
the distance of each judgment from such region. In the case of
S_{b}
and
S_{b}^{\infty}
,
there is no shared path that leads to a region where no differences are
detectable.

\lrcorner

Example 3.2.2
.

Consider the session types

\begin{array}[]{rcl}S&=&{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}play}.({!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}win}.S+{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}lose}.S)+{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}quit}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\\
T&=&{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}play}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}lose}.T+{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}quit}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\end{array}

describing
the behavior of two slot machines, an unbiased one in which the player may win
at every play and a biased one in which the player never wins. If we try to
build a derivation for
S\leqslant_{n}T
we obtain

\displaystyle\penalty\mathstrut\smash{\vdots}

\displaystyle S\leqslant_{n-1}T

[f-tag-out-1]

\displaystyle{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}win}.S+{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}lose}.S\leqslant_{n}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}lose}.T

[f-end]

\displaystyle{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{n}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}

[f-tag-in]

\displaystyle S\leqslant_{n}T

which would contain an infinite branch with strictly decreasing annotations.
Therefore, we have
S\not\leqslant T
.
In this case there exists a shared path leading into a region of
S
and
T

in which no more differences are detectable between the two protocols, but
this path starts from an input. The fact that
S
is
not
a fair subtype
of
T
has a semantic justification. Think of a player that deliberately
insists on playing until it wins. This is possible when the player interacts
with the unbiased slot machine
S
but not with the biased one
T
.

\lrcorner

Now we can provide a semantic characterization of fair subtyping for binary and multiparty
session types as the relation preserving
compliance
(
Definition

2.3.1
) and

coherence
(
Definition

2.3.2
) of the involved session, respectively.
Since the notion of
coherence
boils down to
compatibility
when there
are exactly two participants, we only state the property in the multiparty scenario.

Definition 3.2.1

(Semantic fair subtyping - Multiparty)
.

We say that
S
is a
fair subtype
of
T
, notation

S\sqsubseteq T
, if
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S

coherent
implies

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright T

coherent
for every
M
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
.

We conclude this section by stating and proving a fundamental property of
fair subtyping.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

\leqslant
is a preorder.

While reflexivity of
\leqslant
is trivial to prove (since
[fsm-tag-out-2]
is
never necessary, it suffices to only consider judgments with a
0
annotation),
transitivity is surprisingly complex. The challenging part of proving that from

S\leqslant_{m}U
and
U\leqslant_{n}T
we can derive
S\leqslant_{k}T
is to come up
with a feasible annotation
k
. As it turns out, such
k
depends not only on

m
and
n
, but also on annotations found in different regions of the
derivation trees that prove
S\leqslant_{m}U
and
U\leqslant_{n}T
. In particular,
the “difference” of
S
and
T
is not simply the “maximum difference” or
“the sum of the differences” of
S
and
U
and of
U
and
T
.
More in detail, we first show that we can always find a derivation of
S\leqslant_{m}U
where the rank annotations of all judgements occurring in it are below some

h\geq m
; then, the judgement
S\leqslant_{k}T
is provable for
k=m+(1+h)n
.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
S\leqslant_{n}^{m}T
if and only if
S\leqslant_{n}T
is the
conclusion of a derivation in which every rank annotation is at
most
m
. Then
S\leqslant_{n}T
if and only
S\leqslant_{n}^{m}T
for
some
m
.

Proof.

The “if” part is obvious. Concerning the “only if” part, it
suffices to show that each judgment in the set

\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\{U\leqslant_{m}V\mid U\leqslant_{m}V\wedge\nexists n<m:U\leqslant_{n}V\}

is derivable from premises that are also in
\mathcal{S}
. This is
enough to prove
S\leqslant_{n}^{m}T
from
S\leqslant_{n}T
, because in

\mathcal{S}
there is at most one judgment
U\leqslant_{n}V
for each pair
of session types
U
and
V
and, by regularity of
U
and
V
,
the derivation of
U\leqslant_{n}V
obtained using judgments in

\mathcal{S}
contains finitely many annotations, which must have a
maximum.

Suppose
U\leqslant_{m}V\in\mathcal{S}
. Then
U\leqslant_{m}V
is
derivable. We reason by cases on the last rule applied to derive
this judgment.

Case
[fsm-end]
.
Then
U=V=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
and there is nothing left to prove since

[fsm-end]
has no premises.

Case
[fsm-tag-in]
.
Then
U=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}
and

V=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.V_{i}
and
I\subseteq J
and

U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}V_{i}
and
n_{i}\leq m
for every
i\in I
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that, for every
i\in I
, there
exists
m_{i}\leq n_{i}
such that
U\leqslant_{m_{i}}V_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
.
Then
U\leqslant_{m}V
is derivable by
[fsm-tag-in]
using
premises in
\mathcal{S}
.

Case
[fsm-tag-out-1]
.
Analogous to the previous case.

Case
[fsm-tag-out-2]
.
Then
U=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}
and

V=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.V_{i}
and
J\subseteq I
and

U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}V_{i}
for every
i\in J
and
n_{k}<m
for some

k\in I
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that, for every
i\in J
, there
exists
m_{i}\leq n_{i}
such that
U\leqslant_{m_{i}}V_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
.
In particular,
m_{k}\leq n_{k}<m
.
Then
U\leqslant_{m}V
is derivable by
[fsm-tag-out-2]
using
premises in
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.2.1
.

The proof that
\leqslant
is reflexive is trivial, since

S\leqslant_{n}S
is derivable for every
n
. Concerning
transitivity, by
lemma

3.2.1
it suffices to show
that each judgment in the set

\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\{S\leqslant_{n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}}T\mid S\leqslant_{n_{1}}^{m}U\wedge U\leqslant_{n_{2}}T\}

is derivable using the rules in
Figure

3.3
from premises that
are also in
\mathcal{S}
.
Suppose
S\leqslant_{n}T\in\mathcal{S}
. Then there exist
U
,
n_{1}
,
m

and
n_{2}
such that
S\leqslant_{n_{1}}^{m}U
and
U\leqslant_{n_{2}}T
and

n=n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}
.
We reason by cases on the last rules applied to derive

S\leqslant_{n_{1}}U
and
U\leqslant_{n_{2}}T
.

Case
[fsm-end]
.
Then
S=U=T=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
hence
S\leqslant_{n}T
is derivable by
[fsm-end]
.

Case
[fsm-tag-in]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and
U=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}

and
T=\sum_{i\in K}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
I\subseteq J\subseteq K
and

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}}^{m}U_{i}
and
n_{1i}\leq n_{1}
for every
i\in I
and
U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{2i}}T_{i}
and
n_{2i}\leq n_{2}
for every
i\in J
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that
S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every
i\in I
.
Observe that
n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}\leq n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}=n
for every

i\in I
hence
S\leqslant_{n}T
is derivable by
[fsm-tag-in]
.

Case
[fsm-tag-out-1]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

U=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}
and
T=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}

and
S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}}^{m}U_{i}
and
n_{1i}\leq n_{1}
and

U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{2i}}T_{i}
and
n_{2i}\leq n_{2}
for every
i\in I
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every

i\in I
.
Observe that
n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}\leq n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}=n

for every
i\in I
hence
S\leqslant_{n}T
is derivable by

[fsm-tag-out-1]
.

Case
[fsm-tag-out-1]
and
[fsm-tag-out-2]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

U=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
J\subseteq I
and

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}}^{m}U_{i}
and
n_{1i}\leq n_{1}
for every
i\in I

and
U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{2i}}T_{i}
for every
i\in J
and
n_{2k}<n_{2}

for some
k\in J
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every

i\in J
.
Observe that
n_{1k}+(1+m)n_{2k}<n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}=n
hence

S\leqslant_{n}T
is derivable by
[f-tag-out-2]
.

Case
[fsm-tag-out-2]
and
[fsm-tag-out-1]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

U=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
J\subseteq I
and

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}}^{m}U_{i}
for every
i\in J
and
n_{1k}<n_{1}

for some
k\in J
and
U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{2i}}T_{i}
for every
i\in J

and
n_{2i}\leq n_{2}
for every
i\in J
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every

i\in J
.
Observe that
n_{1k}+(1+m)n_{2k}<n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}=n
hence

S\leqslant_{n}T
is derivable by
[fsm-tag-out-2]
.

Case
[fsm-tag-out-2]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
,

U=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.U_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in K}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and

K\subseteq J\subseteq I
and
S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}}^{m}U_{i}
for
every
i\in J
and
n_{1j}<n_{1}
for some
j\in J
and

U_{i}\leqslant_{n_{2i}}T_{i}
for every
i\in K
and
n_{2k}<n_{2}
for
some
k\in K
.
By definition of
\mathcal{S}
we have that

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{1i}+(1+m)n_{2i}}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every

i\in K
.
Observe that

\begin{array}[]{rcll}n_{1k}+(1+m)n_{2k}&\leq&m+(1+m)n_{2k}&\text{since $n_{1k}\leq m$}\\
&<&1+m+(1+m)n_{2k}\\
&=&(1+m)(1+n_{2k})\\
&\leq&(1+m)n_{2}&\text{since $n_{2k}<n_{2}$}\\
&<&n_{1}+(1+m)n_{2}&\text{since $n_{1j}<n_{1}$}\end{array}

hence
S\leqslant_{n}T
is derivable by
[fsm-tag-out-2]
.
∎

3.2.1
Correctness - Soundness

Now we prove the
soundness
of
\leqslant
with respect
to
\sqsubseteq
(
Definition

3.2.1
). That is, we prove that
\leqslant

is
coherence
-preserving just like
\sqsubseteq
is.
The proof of this result relies on a key property of
\leqslant
not enjoyed by the
usual subtyping relation on session types
[
Gay and Hole 2005
]
: when
S\leqslant T
and

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S
is coherent, the session map
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright T
can
successfully terminate (
Lemma

3.2.3
).
The rank annotation on subtyping judgements is used to
set up an appropriate inductive argument for proving this property.

Theorem 3.2.2

(Soundness)
.

If
S\leqslant T
then
S\sqsubseteq T
.

We start with an auxiliary result formalizing the simulation entailed by the
relation
S\leqslant T
.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

If
S\leqslant T
and
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent and
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\Rightarrow N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
, then
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}\Rightarrow N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S^{\prime}}
for some
S^{\prime}\leqslant T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We prove the result for a single reduction

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
. The
general statement then follows by a straightforward induction on
the length of the reduction

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\Rightarrow N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
using the
fact that coherence is preserved by reductions.

Case
M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}N
.
Then
T^{\prime}=T
and we conclude by taking
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}S
.

Case
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime\prime}}
.
Then

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}}.T_{k}}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
for
some
k\in I
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant T
we deduce

S=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
where
I\subseteq J
and

S_{i}\leqslant T_{i}
for every
i\in I
.
Now we have

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.S_{k}}
and also

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.S_{k}\leqslant T^{\prime}
.
We conclude by taking
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.S_{k}
.

Case
M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}}}N
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
.
Then
T=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}
and

T^{\prime}=T_{k}
for some
k\in I
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant T
we deduce

S=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and
J\subseteq I
and

S_{i}\leqslant T_{i}
for every
i\in J
.
From the hypothesis
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
coherent we deduce

k\in J
or else the participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
would not be able to
receive the
m
tag.
We conclude by taking
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}S_{k}
.

Case
M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}}}N
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
.
Then
T=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}
and

T^{\prime}=T_{k}
for some
k\in I
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant T
we deduce

S=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and
I\subseteq J
and

S_{i}\leqslant T_{i}
for every
i\in I
.
We conclude by taking
S^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}S_{k}
.

Case
M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}U}N
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}U}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
.
Then
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}U.T^{\prime}
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant T
we deduce
S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}U.S^{\prime}

and
S^{\prime}\leqslant T^{\prime}
.
We conclude by observing that

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S^{\prime}}
.

Case
M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}U}N
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}U}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
.
Then
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}U.T^{\prime}
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant T
we deduce
S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}U.S^{\prime}

and
S^{\prime}\leqslant T^{\prime}
.
We conclude by observing that

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S^{\prime}}
.
∎

Next we show that
S\leqslant T
preserves the termination of any session map that
completes
S
into a coherent one.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

If
S\leqslant_{n}T
and
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent, then
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{?}\checkmark}}{{\Longrightarrow}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the lexicographically ordered tuple

(n,|\varphi|)
where
\varphi
is any string of actions such
that
M\xRightarrow{\overline{\varphi}\overline{\pi}\checkmark}
and

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}\xRightarrow{\varphi\pi\checkmark}
. We know that at
least one such
\varphi
least does exist from the hypothesis

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent.
We now reason by cases on the shape of
\varphi
.

Case
\varphi=\varepsilon
.
Then
S=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant_{n}T
and
[fsm-end]
we deduce

T=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
and we conclude

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{?}\checkmark}}{{\Longrightarrow}}
.

Case
\varphi=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}\psi
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}
for some

k\in I
.
From the hypothesis
S\leqslant_{n}T
and
[fsm-tag-in]
we
deduce
T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
I\subseteq J

and
S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}
and
n_{i}\leq n
for every
i\in I
.
We conclude using the induction hypothesis.

Case
\varphi=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}\psi
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}
for some

k\in I
. We distinguish two sub-cases, according to the last rule
used in the derivation of
S\leqslant_{n}T
.
If the last rule was
[fsm-tag-out-1]
, then

T=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and
S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}

and
n_{i}\leq n
for every
i\in I
.
In particular,
S_{k}\leqslant_{n_{k}}T_{k}
and
n_{k}\leq n
and we
conclude using the induction hypothesis.
If the last rule was
[fsm-tag-out-2]
, then

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
with
J\subseteq I
and

S_{i}\leqslant_{n_{i}}T_{i}
for every
i\in J
and
n_{j}<n
for some

j\in J
.
In particular, we have
S_{j}\leqslant_{n_{j}}T_{j}
and we conclude using
the induction hypothesis.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.2.2
.

Consider a run

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}\Rightarrow N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
.
From
Lemma

3.2.2
we deduce that there exists
S^{\prime}\leqslant T^{\prime}

such that
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}\Rightarrow N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S^{\prime}}
.
From the hypothesis that
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent we
deduce
N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S^{\prime}}
is also coherent.
From
Lemma

3.2.3
we conclude

N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{?}\checkmark}}{{\Longrightarrow}}
.
∎

3.2.2
Correctness - Completeness

Theorem

3.2.2
alone suffices to justify the adoption of
\leqslant
as
fair subtyping relation, but we are interested in understanding to which extent

\leqslant
covers
\sqsubseteq
. In this respect, it is quite easy to see that there
exist session types that are related by
\sqsubseteq
but not by
\leqslant
. For
example, consider
S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a}.S
and
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}b}.T
and
observe that these two session types describe completely different protocols
(the output of infinitely many
a
’s in the case of
S
and the input of
infinitely many
b
’s in the case of
T
). In particular, we have
S\not\leqslant T
and
T\not\leqslant S
but also
S\sqsubseteq T
and
T\sqsubseteq S
. That
is,
S
and
T
are
unrelated
according to
\leqslant
but they are

equivalent
according to
\sqsubseteq
. This equivalence is justified by the
fact that there exists no coherent session map in which
S
and
T
could play
any role, because none of them can ever terminate.

This discussion hints at the possibility that, if we restrict the attention to
those session types that
can
terminate, which are the interesting ones as
far as this work is concerned, then we can establish a tighter correspondence
between
\leqslant
and
\sqsubseteq
. We call such session types
bounded
, because
they describe protocols for which termination is always within reach.

Definition 3.2.2

(Bounded session type)
.

We say that a session type is
bounded
if all of its subtrees contain a

\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
leaf.

Note that a
finite
session type is always bounded but not every bounded
session type is finite. If we consider the reduction system in which states are
session types and we have
S\to T
if
T
is an immediate subtree of
S
, then

S
is bounded if and only if
S
is fairly terminating.
Now, for the family of bounded session types we can prove a
relative
completeness
result for
\leqslant
with respect to
\sqsubseteq
.

Theorem 3.2.3

(Relative completeness)
.

S
bounded,
S\sqsubseteq T
imply
S\leqslant T
.

The proof of
Theorem

3.2.3
is done by contradiction. We show
that, for any bounded
S
, if
S\leqslant T
does not hold then we can build a
session map
M
called
discriminator
such that
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S
is
coherent and
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright T
is not, which contraddicts the hypothesis
S\sqsubseteq T
.
The boundedness of
S
is necessary to make sure that it is always possible to
find a session map
N
such that
N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S
is coherent.

For this proof we need some auxiliary notions and notation.
First of all, we consider
unfair subtyping

\leqslant_{*}
from

Figure

3.1
and we fix the invariance of the type of the
channel being sent by substituting rules
[us-channel-in]

and
[us-channel-out]
with

\frac{S\leqslant_{*}T}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi U.S\leqslant_{*}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi U.T}\quad\textsc{us-channel}

It is straightforward to see that
{\leqslant}\subseteq{\leqslant_{*}}
.
Then, we introduce some convenient notation for building session maps. To do
this, we assume the existence of an arbitrary total order
<
on the set of
roles.
Now, given a finite set of roles
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{n}\}
where
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{1}<\cdots<\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{n}
, we write
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{n}\}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}.S
for the
session type
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{1}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}\cdots\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{n}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}.S
.
Given a finite family
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of session maps all having the same
domain
\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\}\subseteq D\subseteq\mathsf{Roles}\setminus\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\}
, we write

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\sum_{i\in I}{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.M_{i}}}
for the session map
M
having domain

D
and such that

M(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.D\setminus\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.M_{i}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q})&\text{if $\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}$}\\
\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.M_{i}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r})&\text{if
$\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}\neq\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}$}\end{cases}

for every
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}\in D
.
As suggested by the notation, this session map realizes a conversation in which

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}
first interacts with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
by exchanging a tag
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}
and then it
informs all the other participants about the tag that has been exchanged. This
session map has the property

M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}}\Rightarrow M_{i}

for every
k\in I
.

Similarly, given a session map
N
with domain
D\supseteq\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\}
, we write

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.N}
for the session map
M
with domain
D
such
that

M(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.N(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q})&\text{if $\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}$}\\
N(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r})&\text{if $\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}\in D\setminus\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\}$}\end{cases}

for every
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}\in D
. Note that
M
has the property

M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}}N

The first key step is showing that
S\sqsubseteq T
implies
S\leqslant_{*}T
when
S
is
a bounded session type. That is, unfair subtyping is a
necessary
condition
for fair subtyping to hold.

Lemma 3.2.4
.

If
S
is bounded and
S\sqsubseteq T
then
S\leqslant_{*}T
.

Proof.

Using the coinduction principle (
Proposition

1.2.2
)
it suffices to show that each judgment in the set

\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\{S\leqslant_{*}T\mid\text{$S$ is bounded and $S\sqsubseteq T$}\}

is derivable by the rules in
Figure

3.1
from premises that
satisfy the same property. Let
S\leqslant_{*}T\in\mathcal{S}
. Then
S
is
bounded and
S\sqsubseteq T
.
We reason by cases on the shape of
S
.

Case
S=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
.
Consider
M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\overline{\pi}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}}
and observe that

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S
is coherent. Then
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright T
is
coherent as well, which implies
T=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
.
We conclude by observing that
\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{*}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
is derivable with

[us-end]
.

Case
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
.
Let
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of session maps such that

M_{i}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S_{i}
is coherent for every
i\in I
. Such
family is guaranteed to exist from the hypothesis that
S
is
bounded.
Without loss of generality we may assume that the
M_{i}
all have
the same domain
D\supseteq\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\}
.
Let
M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.M_{i}}
and observe that

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent by definition of
M
. Then

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}
is coherent as well.
We deduce that
T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and

J\subseteq I
and also that
M_{i}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T_{i}}
is coherent
for every
i\in J
. Hence
S_{i}\sqsubseteq T_{i}
for every
i\in J
,
namely
S_{i}\leqslant_{*}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every
i\in J
by definition
of
\mathcal{S}
.
We conclude by observing that
S\leqslant_{*}T
is derivable by

[us-tag-out]
.

Case
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
.
Let
\{M_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be a family of session maps such that

M_{i}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S_{i}}
is coherent for every
i\in I
. Such
family is guaranteed to exist from the hypothesis that
S
is
bounded. Without loss of generality we may assume that the
M_{i}

all have the same domain
D\supseteq\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\}
.
Let
M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.M_{i}}
and observe that

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent by definition of
M
.
We deduce that
T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
and

I\subseteq J
and also that
M_{i}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T_{i}}
is coherent
for every
i\in I
. Hence
S_{i}\sqsubseteq T_{i}
for every
i\in I
,
namely
S_{i}\leqslant_{*}T_{i}\in\mathcal{S}
for every
i\in I
by definition
of
\mathcal{S}
.
We conclude by observing that
S\leqslant_{*}T
is derivable by

[us-tag-in]
.

Case
S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\pi{U}.S^{\prime}
.
Let
N
be a session map such that
N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S^{\prime}}
is
coherent. Such
N
is guaranteed to exist from the hypothesis that

S
is bounded.
Let
M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\overline{\pi}{U}.N}
and observe that

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent by definition of
M
.
We deduce that
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\pi{U}.T^{\prime}
and also that

N\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T^{\prime}}
is coherent. Hence
S^{\prime}\sqsubseteq T^{\prime}
, namely

S^{\prime}\leqslant_{*}T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
by definition of
\mathcal{S}
.
We conclude by observing that
S\leqslant_{*}T
is derivable by

[us-channel]
.
∎

Next we show that every bounded session type may be part of a coherent session
map. This result is somewhat related to the notion of
duality
in binary
session type theories
[
Honda 1993
,
Honda et al. 1998
]
, showing that every
behavior can be completed by a matching – dual – one.

Definition 3.2.3

(Duality)
.

Let
\mathsf{targets}(\cdot)
be the function that yields the set of roles occurring
in a session type, let
S
be a bounded session type and
D
be a non-empty
set of roles that includes
\mathsf{targets}(S)
but not
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
.
Let
\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S)
be the session map corecursively defined by
the following equations:

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}ll}\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end})&=&\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\in D}\\
\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end})&=&\min D\triangleright{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\mathbin{|}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\}_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\in D\setminus\{\min D\}}\\
\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i})&=&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\overline{\pi}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S_{i})\\
\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\pi{U}.S)&=&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\overline{\pi}{U}.\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S)\end{array}

Lemma 3.2.5

(Duality)
.

\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S)\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S
is coherent.

Proof.

Follows from the definition of
\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S)
.
∎

Now we provide an algorithmic way of computing the “difference” between two
session types related by unfair subtyping.

Definition 3.2.4

(Subtyping weight)
.

Under the hypothesis
S\leqslant_{*}T
, let
\mathsf{wg}(S,T)\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}

be the least solution of the system of equations below:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{~}c@{~}ll@{}}\mathsf{wg}(\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end},\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end})&=&0\\
\mathsf{wg}(\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i},\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i})&=&\max_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})&I\subseteq J\\
\mathsf{wg}(\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i},\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i})&=&1+\min_{i\in J}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})&J\subsetneq I\\
\mathsf{wg}(\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i},\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i})&=&\min\{\\
&&~~1+\min_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i}),\\
&&~~\max_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})\}\\
\mathsf{wg}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.S^{\prime},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.T^{\prime})&=&\mathsf{wg}(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\end{array}

To see that
\mathsf{wg}(S,T)
is well defined, observe that the system of equations
defining
\mathsf{wg}(S,T)
under the hypothesis
S\leqslant_{*}T
contains finitely many
equations, say
n
, by regularity of
S
and
T
. The system is representable as
a monotone endofunction
F
on the complete lattice
(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\})^{n}
.
Thus,
F
has a least solution of which
\mathsf{wg}(S,T)
is a component.
We call two session types
S
and
T
divergent if they are related by unfair
subtyping and have infinite rank.

Definition 3.2.5

(Divergence)
.

We write
S\mathrel{\uparrow}T
if
S\leqslant_{*}T
and
\mathsf{wg}(S,T)=\infty
.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

If
S\mathrel{\uparrow}T
then the derivation of
S\leqslant_{*}T
contains at least one application of
[u-tag-out]
with
J\subsetneq I
and one of the following holds:

1.

S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
with
I\subseteq J
and

S_{k}\mathrel{\uparrow}T_{k}
for some
k\in I
, or

2.

S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{j}.T_{j}
with
J\subseteq I
and

S_{i}\mathrel{\uparrow}T_{i}
for every
i\in J
, or

3.

S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.S^{\prime}
and
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.T^{\prime}
and

S^{\prime}\mathrel{\uparrow}T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If the derivation of
S\leqslant_{*}T
contained no application of

[u-tag-out]
with
J\subsetneq I
we would have

\mathsf{wg}(S,T)=0
. Now we reason by cases on the last rule used to
derive
S\leqslant_{*}T
.

Case
[us-end]
.
Then
S=T=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
. This case is impossible because

\mathsf{wg}(\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end},\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end})=0
by definition.

Case
[us-tag-in]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
with
I\subseteq J
and

S_{i}\leqslant_{*}T_{i}
for every
i\in I
and

\infty=\mathsf{wg}(S,T)=\max_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})
.
That is,
\mathsf{wg}(S_{k},T_{k})=\infty
for some
k\in I
, hence we
conclude
S_{k}\mathrel{\uparrow}T_{k}
.

Case
[us-tag-out]
.
Then
S=\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}
and

T=\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}
with
J\subseteq I
and

S_{i}\leqslant_{*}T_{i}
for every
i\in J
.
We distinguish two sub-cases.
If
J\subsetneq I
then

\infty=\mathsf{wg}(S,T)=1+\min_{i\in J}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})
, that is

\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})=\infty
for every
i\in J
.
If
J=I
then

\infty=\mathsf{wg}(S,T)=\min\{1+\min_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i}),\max_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})\}\leq 1+\min_{i\in I}\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})
and we have
\mathsf{wg}(S_{i},T_{i})=\infty
for every
i\in I
.
Therefore, in both cases, we conclude
S_{i}\mathrel{\uparrow}T_{i}
for every

i\in J
.

Case
[us-channel]
.
Then
S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.S^{\prime}
and
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi{U}.T^{\prime}
and

S^{\prime}\leqslant_{*}T^{\prime}
and
\infty=\mathsf{wg}(S,T)=\mathsf{wg}(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})
hence we
conclude
S^{\prime}\mathrel{\uparrow}T^{\prime}
.
∎

Finally, the key aspect of the proof of
Lemma

3.2.7
is
how we build the session map
M
such that
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}

is coherent while
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}
is not.

Definition 3.2.6

(Discriminator)
.

Let
\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S,T)
be the session map
corecursively defined by the following equations:

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}c@{~}l}\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i},\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i})&=&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\sum_{i\in I,S_{i}\mathrel{\uparrow}T_{i}}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S_{i},T_{i})\\
&&\hfill\text{if $I\subseteq J$}\\
\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i},\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i})&=&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S_{i},T_{i})\\
&+&\sum_{i\in I\setminus J}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.\mathsf{dual}_{D}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright S_{i})\\
&&\hfill\text{if $J\subseteq I$}\\
\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\pi U.S,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\pi U.T)&=&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}\triangleright\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\overline{\pi}U.\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S,T)\end{array}

Lemma 3.2.7
.

If
S
is bounded and
S\mathrel{\uparrow}T
then
S\not\sqsubseteq T
.

Proof.

From the hypothesis that

S\mathrel{\uparrow}T
and
Lemma

3.2.6
we deduce that the
derivation of
S\leqslant_{*}T
contains at least one application of

[us-tag-out]
with
J\subsetneq I
.
Consider
\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S,T)
from
Definition

3.2.6
.
Note that
\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S,T)
always sends a subset of
the labels accepted by
S
, it is willing to receive any label
sent by
S
, and it can always terminate successfully when
interacting with
S
. Note also that it terminates successfully
only after receiving a label from
S
that
T
cannot sent.
Therefore, we have

\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S,T)\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
coherent and

\mathsf{disc}(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p},S,T)\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}
incoherent,
which proves
S\not\sqsubseteq T
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.2.3
.

Let
S\leqslant_{@}T
if
S\leqslant_{*}T
and
\mathsf{wg}(U,T)<\infty
for
every judgment
U\leqslant_{*}V
in the derivation of
S\leqslant_{*}T
.
From
Lemmas

3.2.4
and
3.2.7
we have that

S\sqsubseteq T
implies
S\leqslant_{@}T
. Indeed, if there is a
judgment
U\leqslant_{*}V
in the derivation of
S\leqslant_{*}T
such that

\mathsf{wg}(U,V)=\infty
, then it is possible to build a session
M

such that
M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{S}
is coherent and

M\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\triangleright{T}
is not by induction on the minimum depth
of the judgment
U\leqslant_{*}V
in the derivation using the
hypothesis that
S
is bounded and
Lemma

3.2.7
.

Now, using the principle of coinduction, it suffices to show that
each judgment in the set

\mathcal{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\{S\leqslant_{\mathsf{wg}(S,T)}T\mid S\leqslant_{@}T\}

is derivable using one of the rules in
Figure

3.3
whose
premises all belong to
\mathcal{S}
.
∎

3.2.3
Generalized Inference System

The purely
coinductive
characterization of fair subtyping
presented in
Figures

3.2
and
3.3
has been first used in
Ciccone et al. 2022
.
Previous works

[
Padovani 2013
,
Padovani 2016
,
Ciccone and Padovani 2021a
,
Ciccone and Padovani 2022c
]

relied on a different characterization based on a generalized
inference system (see
Section

1.2
) that consists of the same rules
of the original subtyping relation (
Figure

3.1
) equipped with
a corule. The generalized inference system for binary session types
is presented in
Figure

3.4
. Note the invariance in the type
of the channel being exchanged according to
Remark

3.2.1
.
In addition to
\leqslant
, we write
\leqslant_{\mathsf{ind}}
for the
relation defined by the
inductive
interpretation of the same inference
system and
\leqslant_{\mathsf{coind}}
for the relation defined by the
coinductive

interpretation of the inference system in
fig.

3.4

excluding the corule
[fs-converge]
. We introduce the
notions of
paths
and
residual
of a session type.

\frac{\mathstrut}{\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\leqslant_{*}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}}\and\frac{S\leqslant_{*}T}{{!}U.S\leqslant_{*}{!}U.T}\\
\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{*}T_{i}}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{*}\sum_{i\in I\cup J}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\and\frac{\forall i\in I:S_{i}\leqslant_{*}T_{i}}{\textstyle\sum_{i\in I\cup J}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\leqslant_{*}\sum_{i\in I}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.T_{i}}\and{\frac{\forall\varphi\in\mathsf{paths}(S)\setminus\mathsf{paths}(T):\exists\psi\leq\varphi,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}:S(\psi{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m})\leqslant T(\psi{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m})}{S\leqslant T}\quad\textsc{fs-converge}}\hypertarget{rule:fs-converge}{}

Figure 3.4:

Generalized inference system for fair subtyping

Definition 3.2.7

(Paths of a session type)
.

We say that
\varphi
is a
path
of
S
if
S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\Longrightarrow}}
. We write

\mathsf{paths}(S)
for the (prefix-closed) set of paths of
S
, that is
\mathsf{paths}(S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\{\varphi\mid S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\}
.

Definition 3.2.8

(Residual of a session type)
.

The
residual
of a session type
S
with respect to a path
\varphi\in\mathsf{paths}(S)
, denoted by
S(\varphi)
, is the unique session type
T
such that

S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\Longrightarrow}}T
.

The subtle difference between fair and unfair subtyping is due to the corule

[fs-converge]
. Since this corule is somewhat obscure,
we explain it gradually starting with the following observations:

1.

Recall from
Section

1.2
that
S\leqslant T
implies
S\leqslant_{\mathsf{coind}}T
and
S\leqslant_{\mathsf{ind}}T
. Hence,
\leqslant
is a refinement of
\leqslant_{\mathsf{coind}}
such that, for each pair
of related session types
S
and
T
, there exists a
finite-depth

derivation tree for the judgment
S\leqslant T
using the rules and possibly the
corule
[fs-converge]
.

2.

When
S\leqslant_{\mathsf{coind}}T
holds, it is not possible to establish a general
correlation between
\mathsf{paths}(S)
and
\mathsf{paths}(T)
. Indeed,
[us-tag-in]

entails that some paths of
T
may not be present in
S
and

[us-tag-out]
entails that some paths of
S
may not be present in
T
.

3.

The judgment
S\leqslant T
is trivially derivable using
[fs-converge]

if the path inclusion relation
\mathsf{paths}(S)\subseteq\mathsf{paths}(T)
holds. Since

[us-tag-out]
is the only rule that allows
T
to have fewer paths
than
S
, we deduce that
[fs-converge]
limits (but does not always
forbid) applications of
[us-tag-out]
.

4.

In general
[fs-converge]
requires that, whenever a path
\varphi

of
S
is no longer present in
T
, it must be possible to find a prefix

\psi
of
\varphi
and an output
{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}
shared by both
S
and
T

such that
S(\psi{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m})
and
T(\psi{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m})
are one step closer to
the region of
S
and
T
where path inclusion holds.

The reason why path inclusion plays such an important role in the definition of
fair subtyping is that a process using a channel of type
T
keeps using it
according to
T
even if it is replaced by another channel of type
S\leqslant T
,
without even realizing that the replacement has taken place. After all, this is
what the “safe substitution principle” (
Proposition

3.1.1
) is based on.
As a consequence, none of
the paths in
S
that have disappeared in
T
will be offered to the process at
the other end of the session. If there are “too few” paths in
T
compared
to
S
, then the replacement might compromise the termination of the process at
the other end of the session, should it crucially rely on those paths to
terminate.
When
S\leqslant T
(and therefore
S\leqslant_{\mathsf{ind}}T
) holds, the corule

[fs-converge]
makes sure that the process using the channel of type
S

believing
that it has type
T
is always at
finite distance
from the region where
path inclusion between (some subtrees of)
S
and (the corresponding subtrees
of)
T
holds. Moreover, this region is always reachable by means of

output actions
(those
{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}
mentioned in
[fs-converge]
) which
are performed actively by the process using the channel. In other words, the process
using such channel is always able, in a finite amount of time and relying on choices and
actions it can perform autonomously, to steer the interaction towards a region
of the protocol where path inclusion holds, hence where a common path to session
termination is guaranteed to exist.

To conclude, the generalized inference system in
Figure

3.4

has the advantage of highlighting the
liveness
-preserving
feature of fair subtyping by using
[fs-converge]
. However,
the purely coinductive characterization that we gave in
Section

3.2

allowed us to to provide a direct proof of
Theorem

3.2.1
.
Indeed, in previous works

[
Padovani 2013
,
Padovani 2016
,
Ciccone and Padovani 2021a
,
Ciccone and Padovani 2022c
]
,
transitivity has been established indirectly by relating the
generalized inference system of
fair subtyping with its semantic definition (
Definition

3.2.1
).

In
Part

III
we will characterize properties of session types
mixing safety and liveness aspects. Starting from the definitions
of the safety (coinductive) parts, we will show how to use corules
to obtain the desired predicates. Hence, for what concerns
fair subtyping, we will refer to
Figure

3.4
to provide
a sound and complete Agda mechanization of such predicate (see
Section

8.5
).

Part II
Enforcing

Fair Termination

(Largo)

Chapter 4
Fair Termination Of Binary Sessions

In this chapter we present the first type system for enforcing fair
termination of
binary sessions
. Although in
Chapter

5
we will
present the same approach applied to multiparty ones, hence to a more general context,
the binary case succeeds in highlighting the main requirements, properties and
challenges of such a type system.
For this reason, in this chapter we focus on paradigmatic examples in order
to guide the reader across the main developed features.
In
Sections

5.1.3
and
5.2.2
we will show some more involved scenarios.
It is worth noting that the proof technique that we adopt to prove the soundness
of the type system is shared by both the binary and the multiparty case. However,
the two proofs differ in some technicalities (
e.g.
deadlock freedom).
The type system that we present in this chapter is a refined version of the one
used by
Ciccone and Padovani 2022c
. Indeed,
Ciccone and Padovani 2022c
rely on
the characterization of fair subtyping presented in
Section

3.2.3
.
For the sake of uniformity with respect to
Ciccone et al. 2022
on which

Chapter

5
will be based on,
we refer to the purely coinductive characterization presented in
Section

3.2
.

The chapter is organized as follows.
In
Section

4.1
we present the syntax and the semantics of the
session based calculus on which we apply static analysis.

Section

4.2
shows the type system for such calculus and introduce
some additional properties that are required to enforce fair termination.
Finally, in
Section

4.3
we detail the soundness proof the type system.

Concerning a comparison of the type system we show in this chapter with respect
to existing works, we delay the discussion to
Chapter

5
.

4.1
Calculus

In this section we introduce the calculus for binary sessions.
We recall some basic notions.
We use an infinite set of
channel names
ranged over by
x
,
y
,
z
,
a set of
message tags
ranged over by
m
, and a set of
process names
ranged over by

A
,
B
,
C
.
We write
\overline{x}
for a possibly empty sequences of
channels, extending this notation to other entities and
we use
\pi
to range over the elements of the set
\{\mathsf{?},\mathsf{!}\}
of

polarities
to distinguish input actions (
\mathsf{?}
) from output actions
(
\mathsf{!}
).

4.1.1
Syntax of Processes

A
program
is a finite set of definitions of the form

A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P
, at most one for each process name, where

P
is a
process
generated by the grammar in
Figure

4.1
.

\begin{array}[t]{@{}rcll@{}}P,Q&::=&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}&\text{termination}\\
&|&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.{P}&\text{signal in}\\
&|&x{{?}}(y).{P}&\text{channel in}\\
&|&{x}{\pi}\{{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}}.{P_{i}}\}_{i\in I}&\text{label in/out}\\
&|&(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{session}\end{array}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}rcll@{}}&|&A\langle\overline{x}\rangle&\text{invocation}\\
&|&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x&\text{signal out}\\
&|&x{{!}}(y).{P}&\text{channel out}\\
&|&P\oplus Q&\text{choice}\\
&|&\lceil x\rceil{P}&\text{cast}\end{array}

Figure 4.1:
Syntax of processes

The process
done
is terminated and performs no action.
The invocation
A\langle\overline{x}\rangle
behaves as
P
if
A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P
is the definition of
A
.
When
\overline{x}
is empty, we write
A
and
A\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P

instead of
A
and
A({})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P
.
The process
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.{P}
waits for a signal from channel
x
indicating that the
session
x
is being closed and then continues as
P
. The process
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x

sends the termination signal on
x
.
The process
x{{?}}(y).{P}
receives a channel
y
from channel
x
and then continues as
P
.
Dually,
x{{!}}(y).{P}
sends
y
on
x
and then continues as
P
.
The process
x\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
exchanges a tag
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}
on channel
x
and then continues as
P_{i}
.
As for session types, we assume that the set
I
in these forms is always non-empty and
that
i\neq j
implies
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}\neq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{j}
for every
i,j\in I
.
Also, we write
x\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}
instead of
x\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
when
I
is a singleton
\{i\}
.
A non-deterministic choice
P_{1}\oplus P_{2}
reduces to either
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
.
The annotation
k\in\{1,2\}
has no operational meaning, it is only used to
record that
P_{k}
leads to the termination of the process and is omitted when
irrelevant.
A session
(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)
is the parallel composition of
P
and
Q
connected by
x
.
Note that composition and restriction is atomic. This approach is inspired by linear-logic
based calculi; we provide more details later on.
Finally, a
cast

\lceil x\rceil P
behaves exactly as
P
. This form simply
records the fact that the type of
x
is subject to an application of fair
subtyping in the typing derivation for
P
. As we will see in

Section

4.2
, we use this form to precisely account for
all places in (the typing derivation of) a process where fair subtyping is used.
Occasionally we write
\lceil x_{1}\cdots x_{n}\rceil P
for
\lceil x_{1}\rceil\cdots\lceil x_{n}\rceil P
.

The only binders are channel inputs
x{{?}}(y).P
and sessions

(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)
. We write
\mathsf{fn}(P)
and
\mathsf{bn}(P)
for the sets of free and bound
channel names occurring in
P
and we identify processes modulo renaming of
bound names.
The program
\mathcal{P}
that provides the meaning to the process names occurring
in processes is often left implicit. Sometimes we write a process definition

A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P
as a proposition or side condition, intending that
such definition is part of the implicit program
\mathcal{P}
.
Note that this approach differs from the one used by
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
as they
introduce process definition in the syntax. We adopt a different approach in order
to keep the calculus as light as possible.

Example 4.1.1
.

Let us revisit and complete the example we sketched in
Example

2.1.1
.
We let the
buyer

add
an
odd
number of items.
We can model the whole system as the following set of process definitions:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{~}l@{}}\textit{Main}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}&(y)((x)(\lceil x\rceil\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\textit{Buyer}(x)\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}&x{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.{x{{!}}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}:\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}:\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}}\\
\textit{Seller}(x,y)\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}&x{{?}}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}:\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}:\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.{y{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,y}}\}\\
\textit{Carrier}(y)\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}&y{{?}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}}}\end{array}

Note that
Buyer
deterministically sends
add
to
Seller
as the
first message, whereas he chooses among
add
and
pay
every other
interaction. After
Buyer
has sent
pay
, it closes the session
x

with
Seller
. At this point,
Seller
sends
ship
to

Carrier
and closes the session
y
.
The cast
\lceil x\rceil
before the invocation of
\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle
in
Main
is meant to
account for the mismatch between the behavior of the buyer, which always
adds an odd number of items to the cart, and that of the business, which
accepts any number of items added to the shopping cart.

\lrcorner

4.1.2
Operational Semantics

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{@{}lr@{~}c@{~}ll@{}}\hypertarget{rule:sb-par-comm}{\textsc{\small[sb-par-comm]}}&(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)&\preccurlyeq&(x)(Q\mathbin{|}P)\\
\hypertarget{rule:sb-par-assoc}{\textsc{\small[sb-par-assoc]}}&(x)(P\mathbin{|}(y)(Q\mathbin{|}R))&\preccurlyeq&(y)((x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)&\text{if $x\in\mathsf{fn}(Q)$}\\
&&&&\text{and $y\not\in\mathsf{fn}(P)$}\\
&&&&\text{and $x\not\in\mathsf{fn}(R)$}\\
\hypertarget{rule:sb-cast-comm}{\textsc{\small[sb-cast-comm]}}&\lceil x\rceil\lceil y\rceil P&\preccurlyeq&\lceil y\rceil\lceil x\rceil P\\
\hypertarget{rule:sb-cast-new}{\textsc{\small[sb-cast-new]}}&(x)(\lceil x\rceil{P}\mathbin{|}Q)&\preccurlyeq&(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)&\\
\hypertarget{rule:sb-cast-swap}{\textsc{\small[sb-cast-swap]}}&(x)(\lceil y\rceil{P}\mathbin{|}Q)&\preccurlyeq&\lceil y\rceil(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{if $x\neq y$}\\
\hypertarget{rule:sb-call}{\textsc{\small[sb-call]}}&A\langle\overline{x}\rangle&\preccurlyeq&P&\text{if $A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P$}\end{array}

Figure 4.2:
Structural pre-congruence of processes

\displaystyle\frac{\mathstrut}{P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to P_{k}}\quad\textsc{rb-choice}~k\in\{1,2\}\hypertarget{rule:rb-choice}{}\and\frac{\mathstrut}{(x)(\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\mathbin{|}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.{P})\to P}\quad\textsc{rb-signal}\hypertarget{rule:rb-signal}{}\and\frac{\mathstrut}{(x)(x{{!}}y.P\mathbin{|}x{{?}}(y).Q)\to(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)}\quad\textsc{rb-channel}\hypertarget{rule:rb-channel}{}\and\frac{\mathstrut}{(x)(x{{!}}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{|}Q)\to(x)(x{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.P_{k}\mathbin{|}Q)}\quad\textsc{rb-pick}~k\in I,|I|>1\hypertarget{rule:rb-pick}{}\and\frac{\mathstrut}{(x)(x{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.P\mathbin{|}x{{?}}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}:Q_{i}\}_{i\in I})\to(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q_{k})}\quad\textsc{rb-tag}~k\in I\hypertarget{rule:rb-tag}{}\and\frac{P\to Q}{(x)(P\mathbin{|}R)\to(x)(Q\mathbin{|}R)}\quad\textsc{rb-par}\hypertarget{rule:rb-tag}{}\and\frac{P\to Q}{\lceil x\rceil P\to\lceil x\rceil Q}\quad\textsc{rb-cast}\hypertarget{rule:rb-cast}{}\and\frac{P\preccurlyeq P^{\prime}\quad P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}\quad Q^{\prime}\preccurlyeq Q}{P\to Q}\quad\textsc{rb-struct}\hypertarget{rule:rb-struct}{}

Figure 4.3:
Reduction of processes

The operational semantics of processes is defined using a structural
pre-congruence relation
\preccurlyeq
and a reduction relation
\to
, both of which
are defined in
Figures

4.2
and
4.3
and described hereafter.
Rules
[sb-par-comm]
and
[sb-par-assoc]
express the usual
commutativity and associativity of parallel composition. In the case of

[sb-par-assoc]
, the side condition
x\in\mathsf{fn}(Q)
makes sure that the
session
(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)
we obtain on the right hand side does indeed connect
P

and
Q
through
x
.
The other side conditions
y\not\in\mathsf{fn}(P)
and
x\not\in\mathsf{fn}(R)
will
always hold when dealing with well-typed processes.
Also note that
[sb-par-assoc]
only describes a right-to-left
associativity of parallel composition and that left-to-right associativity is
derivable by the chain of relations

\begin{array}[]{r@{~}l}(x)((y)(P\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)&\preccurlyeq(x)(R\mathbin{|}(y)(P\mathbin{|}Q))\\
&\preccurlyeq(x)(R\mathbin{|}(y)(Q\mathbin{|}P))\\
&\preccurlyeq(y)((x)(R\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}P)\\
&\preccurlyeq(y)((x)(Q\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{|}P)\preccurlyeq(y)(P\mathbin{|}(x)(Q\mathbin{|}R))\end{array}

when
x\in\mathsf{fn}(Q)
.
Axiom
[sb-cast-new]
annihilates a cast on
x
nearby
the binder for
x
, making sure that casts can only be removed and never added.
Axioms
[sb-cast-comm]
and
[sb-cast-swap]
are used to move casts
closer to their binder so that they can be annihilated with

[sb-cast-new]
. Rule
[sb-call]
unfolds process invocations to their
definition.

Remark 4.1.1

(On structural pre-congruence)
.

The reader might be wonder why we adopted a pre-congruence relation instead of a more common
congruence one. The reason behind this choice is that in general it reduces the number of
cases to be analyzed in some proofs (see
Lemma

4.3.1
) without compromising
the properties of the calculus. Moreover, the adoption of a congruence relation would
lead to some unrealistic cases from the computation point of view. Indeed,

[sb-cast-new]
would introduce a reflexive application of fair subtyping from
a session restriction and
[sb-call]
would fold a process call.

\lrcorner

The reduction rules are mostly unremarkable:
[rb-choice]
models the
non-deterministic choice between alternative behaviors;
[rb-pick]
models
a non-trivial choice among a set of labels to send;
[rb-signal]
,

[rb-label]
and
[rb-channel]
model synchronizations between a
sender (on the left hand side of the parallel composition) and a receiver (on
the right hand side of the parallel composition) with
[rb-signal]

removing the binder of a closed session;
[rb-par]
,
[rb-cast]
and

[rb-struct]
close reductions under parallel compositions, under casts and
by structural pre-congruence.
In the following we write
\Rightarrow
for the reflexive, transitive closure of

\to
and
\Rightarrow^{+}
for
\Rightarrow\to
.

Example 4.1.2
.

With the definitions given in
Example

4.1.1
, it is easy to see that
there is an infinite reduction sequence starting from
Main
in which the
acquirer keeps adding items to the cart:

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{~}l@{}}&(y)((x)(\lceil x\rceil\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\Rightarrow&(y)((x)(x{{!}}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}:\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}:\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\to&(y)((x)(x{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\Rightarrow&(y)((x)(\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\to&\cdots\end{array}

Nonetheless,
Main
is fairly terminating. For example, we have:

\begin{array}[]{c@{~}l}&(y)((x)(x{{!}}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}:\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}:\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\to&(y)((x)(x{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\mathbin{|}\textit{Seller}\langle x,y\rangle)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\Rightarrow&(y)((x)(\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\mathbin{|}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.{.}y{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,y)\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\to&(y)(y{{!}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,y\mathbin{|}\textit{Carrier}\langle y\rangle)\\
\Rightarrow&(y)(\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,y\mathbin{|}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}})\\
\to&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}\end{array}

Note that in general it might be necessary for the acquirer to add one more item to the cart before it can send the payment to the business and the carrier receives a
ship
message.

\lrcorner

4.2
Type System

In this section we present the type system for binary sessions that we introduced in
Section

4.1
.
Before looking at the typing rules we motivate, through a series of examples, the key properties enforced by the type system that,
taken together, guarantee fair termination. There are two families of problems that can compromise fair termination.
First of all, the process (or part thereof) may be unable to reduce further but is not
done
.
In our model, this can happen for many reasons, for example: a process attempts at sending a
tag on a session that the receiver is not willing to accept; a process attempts at sending a
termination signal when the receiver expects a channel; the processes at the two ends of the same
session are both waiting for a message from that session. These are all examples of
safety violations
,
which are prevented by any ordinary session type system.
In
Section

4.2.1
we focus instead on
liveness violations
. Roughly
speaking, liveness is violated when a process (or part thereof) engages an
infinite computation that cannot possibly terminate.

4.2.1
Boundedness Properties

In
Chapter

3

we have introduced a fair subtyping relation that is liveness preserving but,
as we will see in a moment, the adoption of fair subtyping alone is not enough
to rule out all potential liveness violations. The type system must also enforce
three properties that we call
action boundedness
,
session
boundedness
and
cast boundedness
guaranteeing that the overall effort
required to terminate the process is finite. In the rest of the section we
describe informally these properties and we show that violating even just one of
them may compromise fair process termination.

Definition 4.2.1

(Action Boundedness)
.

We say that a process is
action bounded
if there is a finite upper bound
to the number of actions it has to perform in order to terminate. An
action-unbounded process cannot terminate.

Example 4.2.1
.

Compare the following processes

\begin{array}[]{ll}A\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}A\oplus\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}&\qquad B\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}B\oplus B\end{array}

and observe that
A
may always reduce to
done
, whereas
B
can only reduce
forever into itself. So
A
is action bounded whereas
B
is not.

\lrcorner

We consider a
parallel composition action bounded if so are
both
processes composed in
parallel.
Action boundedness is a necessary condition for (fair) process termination,
hence the type system must guarantee that well-typed processes are action
bounded. As we will see in
Section

4.2.2
, this can be easily achieved by means
of
typing corules
(see
Section

1.2
).
Besides, action boundedness carries along two welcome side effects.
The first one is that degenerate process definitions such as
