\Gamma^{\prime},x:T\vdash_{\mathsf{ind}}Q

•

S\leqslant T

Using the induction hypothesis on
\Gamma^{\prime},x:T\vdash_{\mathsf{ind}}Q
we
deduce that there exists
Q^{\mathit{nf}}
such that
Q\preccurlyeq Q^{\mathit{nf}}
.
We conclude by taking
P^{\mathit{nf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\lceil x\rceil Q^{\mathit{nf}}
using the fact that
\preccurlyeq
is a pre-congruence.
∎

In order to show that every well-typed, closed process in thread normal form can
also be rewritten in proximity normal form we prove
Lemma

4.3.9
,
which pushes a restriction
(x)
next to a process in which
x
occurs free,
which might as well be an
x
-thread.

Lemma 4.3.9

(Proximity)
.

If
x\in\mathsf{fn}(P)\setminus\mathsf{bn}(\mathcal{C})
, then
(x)(\mathcal{C}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)\preccurlyeq\mathcal{D}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]
for some
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
\mathcal{C}
and by cases on its shape.

Case
\mathcal{C}=[~]
.
We conclude by taking
\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}[~]
using the reflexivity
of
\preccurlyeq
.

Case
\mathcal{C}=(y)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\mathbin{|}R)
.
From the hypothesis
x\in\mathsf{fn}(P)\setminus\mathsf{bn}(\mathcal{C})

we deduce
x\neq y
and
x\in\mathsf{fn}(P)\setminus\mathsf{bn}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
Using the induction hypothesis we deduce that there exists
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}

such that
(x)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)\preccurlyeq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]
.
Take
\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}(y)(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\mathbin{|}R)
. We conclude

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(x)(\mathcal{C}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&=&(x)((y)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}R)\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{by definition of $\mathcal{C}$}\\
&\preccurlyeq&(x)(Q\mathbin{|}(y)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}R))&\text{by \hyperlink{rule:sb-par-comm}{{\small[sb-par-comm]}}}\\
&\preccurlyeq&(y)((x)(Q\mathbin{|}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P])\mathbin{|}R)&\text{by \hyperlink{rule:sb-par-assoc}{{\small[sb-par-assoc]}}}\\
&&&\text{and $x\in\mathsf{fn}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P])$}\\
&\preccurlyeq&(y)((x)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)\mathbin{|}R)&\text{by \hyperlink{rule:sb-par-comm}{{\small[sb-par-comm]}}}\\
&\preccurlyeq&(y)(\mathcal{D}^{\prime}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]\mathbin{|}R)&\text{by induction hypothesis}\\
&=&\mathcal{D}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]&\text{by definition of $\mathcal{D}$}\end{array}

where, in using
[sb-par-assoc]
, we note that

x\in\mathsf{fn}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P])
since
x\in\mathsf{fn}(P)\setminus\mathsf{bn}(\mathcal{C})
.

Case
\mathcal{C}=(y)(R\mathbin{|}\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
.
Symmetric of the previous case.

Case
\mathcal{C}=\lceil y\rceil\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
x\neq y
.
Using the induction hypothesis we deduce that there exists
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
such that

(x)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)\preccurlyeq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]
.
Take
\mathcal{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\lceil y\rceil\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
. We conclude

\begin{array}[]{rcll}(x)(\mathcal{C}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&=&(x)(\lceil y\rceil\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{by definition of $\mathcal{C}$}\\
&\preccurlyeq&\lceil y\rceil(x)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{by \hyperlink{rule:sb-cast-swap}{{\small[sb-cast-swap]}} and $x\neq y$}\\
&\preccurlyeq&\lceil y\rceil\mathcal{D}^{\prime}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]&\text{using the induction hypothesis}\\
&=&\mathcal{D}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]&\text{by definition of $\mathcal{D}$}\end{array}

Case
\mathcal{C}=\lceil x\rceil\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
Using the induction hypothesis we deduce that there exists
\mathcal{D}
such that

(x)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)\preccurlyeq\mathcal{D}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]
.
We conclude

\begin{array}[b]{rcll}(x)(\mathcal{C}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&=&(x)(\lceil x\rceil\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{by definition of $\mathcal{C}$}\\
&\preccurlyeq&(x)(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}[P]\mathbin{|}Q)&\text{by \hyperlink{rule:sb-cast-new}{{\small[sb-cast-new]}}}\\
&\preccurlyeq&\mathcal{D}[(x)(P\mathbin{|}Q)]&\text{using the induction hypothesis}\end{array}\qed

We can now prove the fact that every well-typed, closed process in thread normal form can
be rewritten using structural pre-congruence either to
done
or to a process in proximity normal form.
Note that this property the one that, combined with the
compatibility
of the involved session,
guarantees deadlock freedom.

Lemma 4.3.10

(Quasi Deadlock Freedom)
.

If
\emptyset\vDash^{\mu}P^{\mathit{nf}}
, then
P^{\mathit{nf}}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
or
P^{\mathit{nf}}\preccurlyeq Q^{\mathit{nf}}
for some
Q^{\mathit{nf}}
in proximity normal form.

Proof.

A simple induction on the derivation of
\emptyset\vDash^{\mu}P^{\mathit{nf}}

allows us to deduce that
P^{\mathit{nf}}
consists of
k
sessions and
k+1
threads.
If
k=0
, then we conclude
P^{\mathit{nf}}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
.
If
k>0
, then each of the
k+1
threads is an
x_{i}
-thread for some

x_{i}
. Since there are
k+1
threads but only
k
distinct sessions, it must
be the case that
x_{i}=x_{j}
for some
1\leq i<j\leq k+1
. In other
words, there exist
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathcal{C}_{2}
,
P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}
and
P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}
such
that
P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}
and
P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}
are
x
-threads and
P^{\mathit{nf}}=\mathcal{C}[(x)(\mathcal{C}_{1}[P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}]\mathbin{|}\mathcal{C}_{2}[P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}])]
.
We conclude

\begin{array}[]{rcll}P^{\mathit{nf}}&=&\mathcal{C}[(x)(\mathcal{C}_{1}[P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}]\mathbin{|}\mathcal{C}_{2}[P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}])]&\text{by definition of $P^{\mathit{nf}}$}\\
&\preccurlyeq&\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{D}_{1}[(x)(P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}\mathbin{|}\mathcal{C}_{2}[P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}])]]&\text{for some $\mathcal{D}_{1}$ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:proximity_bin}}\\
&\preccurlyeq&\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{D}_{1}[(x)(\mathcal{C}_{2}[P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}]\mathbin{|}P^{\mathit{th}}_{1})]]&\text{by \hyperlink{rule:s-par-comm}{{\small[s-par-comm]}}}\\
&\preccurlyeq&\mathcal{C}[\mathcal{D}_{1}[\mathcal{D}_{2}[(x)(P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}\mathbin{|}P^{\mathit{th}}_{1})]]]&\text{for some $\mathcal{D}_{2}$ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:proximity_bin}}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}&Q^{\mathit{nf}}\end{array}

where
x=x_{i}=x_{j}
. The fact that
Q^{\mathit{nf}}
is in thread normal form follows
from the observation that
P^{\mathit{nf}}
does not have unguarded casts (it is a closed process in thread normal form)
so the pre-congruence rules applied here and in
Lemma

4.3.9
do not move casts around.
We conclude that
Q^{\mathit{nf}}
is in proximity normal form by its shape.
∎

4.3.4
Soundness

Here we prove the soundness of the type system (see
Theorem

4.2.1
). As already hinted at in

Section

4.2.2
, the proof is loosely based on the method of helpful directions

[
Francez 1986
]
, namely on the property that a (well-typed) process
may

reduce in such a way that its measure strictly decreases. Recall that this
property is not true for every reduction. Here we assume that the reducing
process is in proximity normal form. The same result will be generalized later
on.

Lemma 4.3.11
.

If
\Gamma\vDash^{\mu}P^{\mathit{nf}}
where
P^{\mathit{nf}}
is in proximity normal
form, then there exist
Q
and
\nu<\mu
such that
P^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q
and
\Gamma\vDash^{\nu}Q
.

Proof.

From the hypothesis that
P^{\mathit{nf}}
is in proximity normal form we know that

P^{\mathit{nf}}=\mathcal{C}[(x)(P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}\mathbin{|}P^{\mathit{th}}_{2})]
for some
\mathcal{C}
,
x
and
P^{\mathit{th}}_{1},P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}

x
-threads.
We reason by induction on
\mathcal{C}
and by cases on its shape.

Case
\mathcal{C}=[~]
.
From
[mtb-thread]
and
[mtb-par]
we deduce that there exist
\Gamma_{i},S_{i},n_{i}
for
i=1,2
such that

•

\Gamma=\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}

•

\mu=(n_{1}+n_{2},\|S_{1},S_{2}\|)

•

\Gamma_{1},x:S_{1}\vdash^{n_{1}}P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}

•

\Gamma_{2},x:S_{2}\vdash^{n_{2}}P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}

•

S_{1}\sim S_{2}

From the hypothesis that
S_{1}\sim S_{2}
we deduce

S_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\varphi}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\overline{\pi}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end},S_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
for some
\varphi
.
We now reason on the rank of the session and on the shape of
S_{i}
.
For the sake of simplicity, we implicitly apply
[sb-par-comm]
at process level.

If
\|S_{1},S_{2}\|=0
, then
S_{1}=\overline{\pi}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
and
S_{2}=\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
for some
\pi
.

•

Case
S_{1}={?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
and
S_{2}={!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
.
Then

–

\Gamma_{2}=\emptyset
and
P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x

–

P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.{Q}

–

\Gamma_{1}\vdash^{n_{1}}Q

From
Lemma

4.3.3
we deduce that
\Gamma_{1}\vDash^{\nu}Q
for some
\nu\leq(n_{1},0)
.
We conclude observing that
P^{\mathit{nf}}\to Q
by
[rb-signal]
and that

\nu\leq(n_{1},0)<(n_{1}+n_{2},\|S_{1},S_{2}\|)=\mu
.

If
\|S_{1},S_{2}\|>1
, then
{S_{1}\mathbin{|}S_{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\overline{\pi}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\mathbin{|}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}}

first using
[lb-tau-l]
/
[lb-tau-r]
and
[lb-sync]
.
Observe that
[lb-pick]
is never used since we are considering the minimum reduction sequence;
a synchronization through
[lb-pick]
and
[lb-sync]
would lead to a longer reduction. T
hen
S_{1}\xrightarrow{{!}{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}}}
and
S_{2}\xrightarrow{{?}{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}}}
for some

\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}
or
S_{1}\xrightarrow{{!}U}
and
S_{2}\xrightarrow{{?}U}
for some
U
.

•

Case
S_{1}=\sum_{i\in I}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S^{\prime}_{i}
and
S_{2}=\sum_{j\in J}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{j}.T_{j}
with
k\in I
.
From the hypothesis that
S_{1}\sim S_{2}
we deduce
I\subseteq J
.
From
[tb-tag]
we deduce that

–

P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}=x\mathsf{!}\{{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}}.{P^{\prime}_{i}}\}_{i\in I}

–

P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}=x\mathsf{?}\{{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{j}}.{Q_{j}}\}_{j\in J}

–

\Gamma_{1},x:S^{\prime}_{i}\vdash^{n_{1}}P^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i\in I

–

\Gamma_{2},x:T_{j}\vdash^{n_{2}}Q_{j}
for all
j\in J

Let
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}(x)(P^{\prime}_{k}\mathbin{|}Q_{k})
and observe that
P^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q
by
[rb-pick]
and
[rb-tag]
.
From
Lemma

5.3.4
we deduce that there exist
\mu_{1}\leq(n_{1},0),\mu_{2}\leq(n_{2},0)
such that

–

x:S^{\prime}_{k}\vDash^{\mu_{1}}P^{\prime}_{k}

–

x:T_{k}\vDash^{\mu_{2}}Q_{k}

Let

\nu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+(0,\|S^{\prime}_{k},T_{k}\|)
.
We conclude with one application of
[mtb-par]
observing that

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\nu&=&\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+(0,\|S^{\prime}_{k},T_{k}\|)&\text{by def. of $\nu$}\\
&\leq&(n_{1}+n_{2},\|S^{\prime}_{k},T_{k}\|)&\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:measure_rank_bin}}\\
&<&(n_{1}+n_{2},\|S_{1},S_{2}\|)&\text{before $\to$}\\
&=&\mu\end{array}

•

Case
S_{1}={!}{S}.T_{1}
and
S_{2}={?}{S}.T_{2}
.

From the hypothesis that
S_{1}\sim S_{2}
and
Definition

2.3.1
we deduce

T_{1}\sim T_{2}
and from

[tb-channel-out]
and
[tb-channel-in]
we deduce that

•

P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}=x{{!}}y.{P^{\prime}_{1}}

•

P^{\mathit{th}}_{2}=x{{?}}y.{P^{\prime}_{2}}

•

\Gamma_{1},x:T_{1}\vdash^{n_{1}}P^{\prime}_{1}

•

\Gamma_{2},x:T_{2},y:S\vdash^{n_{2}}P^{\prime}_{2}

Let
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}(x)(P^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{|}P^{\prime}_{2})
and observe that
P^{\mathit{nf}}\to Q
by
[rb-channel]
.
From
Lemma

4.3.3
we deduce that there exist
\mu_{1}\leq(n_{1},0),\mu_{2}\leq(n_{2},0)
such that

•

\Gamma_{1},x:T_{1}\vDash^{\mu_{1}}P^{\prime}_{1}

•

\Gamma_{2},x,y:S\vDash^{\mu_{2}}P^{\prime}_{2}

Let
\nu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+(0,\|T_{1},T_{2}\|)
.
We conclude with one application of
[mtb-par]
observing that

\begin{array}[]{rcll}\nu&=&\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+(0,\|T_{1},T_{2}\|)&\text{by definition of $\nu$}\\
&\leq&(n_{1}+n_{2},\|T_{1},T_{2}\|)&\text{by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:measure_rank_multi}}\\
&<&(n_{1}+n_{2},\|S_{1},S_{2}\|)&\text{before reductions}\\
&=&\mu\end{array}

Case
\mathcal{C}=(y)(\mathcal{D}\mathbin{|}Q^{\mathit{par}})
.
Let
R^{\mathit{nf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{D}[(x)(P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}\mathbin{|}P^{\mathit{th}}_{2})]
and observe that
R^{\mathit{nf}}
is in proximity normal form.
From
[mtb-par]
we deduce that there exist
\Gamma_{i},S_{i},\mu_{i}
for
i=1,2
such that

•

\Gamma=\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}

•

\Gamma_{1},y:S_{1}\vDash^{\mu_{1}}R^{\mathit{nf}}

•

\Gamma_{2},y:S_{2}\vDash^{\mu_{2}}Q^{\mathit{par}}

•

\mu=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+(0,\|S_{1},S_{2}\|)

Using the induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{1},y:S_{1}\vDash^{\mu_{1}}R^{\mathit{nf}}
we deduce that there
exists
Q^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}<\mu_{1}
such that

•

R^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q^{\prime}

•

\Gamma_{1},y:S_{1}\vDash^{\nu^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}

We conclude taking
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}(t)(Q^{\prime}\mathbin{|}Q^{\mathit{par}})
and

\nu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\nu^{\prime}+\mu_{2}+(0,\|S_{1},S_{2}\|)

and observing that
\nu<\mu
and
P^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q
by
[rb-par]
.

Case
\mathcal{C}=(x)(Q^{\mathit{par}}\mathbin{|}\mathcal{D})
.
Symmetric to the previous case.

Case
\mathcal{C}=\lceil y\rceil{\mathcal{D}}
.
Observe that
x\neq y
. Let
R^{\mathit{nf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{D}[(x)(P^{\mathit{th}}_{1}\mathbin{|}P^{\mathit{th}}_{2})]

and note that
R^{\mathit{nf}}
is in proximity normal form.
From
[mtb-cast]
we deduce that there exists
\Delta,\mu^{\prime},S,T,m_{y}
such that

•

\Gamma=\Delta,y:S

•

S\leqslant_{m_{y}}T

•

\mu=\mu^{\prime}+(m_{y},0)

•

\Delta,y:T\vDash^{\mu^{\prime}}R^{\mathit{nf}}

Using the induction hypothesis on
\Delta,y:T\vDash^{\mu^{\prime}}R^{\mathit{nf}}
we deduce that
there exist
Q^{\prime}
and
\nu^{\prime}<\mu^{\prime}
such that
R^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q^{\prime}
and
\Delta,y:T\vDash^{\nu^{\prime}}Q^{\prime}
.
We conclude taking
Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\lceil y\rceil{Q^{\prime}}
and
\nu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\tiny\sf def}}}{{=}}\nu^{\prime}+(m_{y},0)

and observing that
\nu<\mu
and
P^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q
by
[rb-cast]
.
∎

Now we prove that any well-typed, closed process can be either rewritten to
done

using structural pre-congruence or reduced so as to obtain a strictly smaller measure.

Lemma 4.3.12
.

If
\emptyset\vDash^{\mu}P
, then either
P\preccurlyeq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
or
P\Rightarrow^{+}Q
and
\emptyset\vDash^{\nu}Q
for some
Q
and
\nu<\mu
.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

4.3.7
we deduce that there exist
P^{\prime}
in choice normal form such that

P\Rightarrow P^{\prime}
and
\emptyset\vDash^{\mu^{\prime}}P^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}\leq\mu
.
By
Lemma

4.3.3
we deduce
\emptyset\vdash P^{\prime}
.
Using
Lemma

4.3.8
we deduce that there exist
P^{\mathit{nf}}
such that
P^{\prime}\preccurlyeq P^{\mathit{nf}}
.
If
P^{\mathit{nf}}=\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
there is nothing left to prove.
If
P^{\mathit{nf}}\neq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
, by
Lemma

4.3.10
we deduce
P^{\mathit{nf}}\preccurlyeq Q^{\mathit{nf}}
for
some
Q^{\mathit{nf}}
in proximity normal form.
From
Lemma

4.3.4
we deduce
\emptyset\vDash^{\mu^{\prime\prime}}Q^{\mathit{nf}}
for some
\mu^{\prime\prime}\leq\mu^{\prime}
.
Using
Lemma

4.3.11
we conclude that
Q^{\mathit{nf}}\Rightarrow^{+}Q
and

\emptyset\vDash^{\nu}Q
for some
Q
and
\nu<\mu^{\prime\prime}\leq\mu^{\prime}\leq\mu
.
∎

Using
Lemma

4.3.12
we can prove that a well-typed, closed process weakly terminates.
Namely, that there exists a finite reduction sequence to
done
.

Lemma 4.3.13

(Weak Termination)
.

If
\emptyset\vdash^{n}P
, then either
P\preccurlyeq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
or
P\Rightarrow^{+}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
.

Proof.

From
Lemma

4.3.3
we deduce that there exists
\mu\leq(n,0)
such that
\emptyset\vDash^{\mu}P
.
We proceed doing an induction on the lexicographically ordered pair
\mu
.
From
Lemma

4.3.12
we deduce either
P\preccurlyeq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
or
P\Rightarrow^{+}Q
and
\emptyset\vDash^{\nu}Q
for some
\nu<\mu
.
In the first case there is nothing left to prove.
In the second case we use the induction hypothesis to deduce that either
Q\preccurlyeq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
or
Q\Rightarrow^{+}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
.
We conclude using either
[rb-struct]
or the transitivity of

\Rightarrow^{+}
, respectively.
∎

The soundness of the type system is now a simple corollary.

Proof of
Theorem

4.2.1
.

Consequence of
Lemma

4.3.2
and
Lemma

4.3.13
.
∎

Chapter 5
Fair Termination Of Multiparty Sessions

In this chapter we generalize the type system in
Chapter

4
to multiparty session types.
In particular, we provide a type system for enforcing (successful) fair termination
of multiparty sessions. In this case we refer to the notion of
coherence
(
Definition

2.3.2
).
Again, we embed
fair subtyping
as the coherence-preserving relation.
Notably, the
boundedness
properties mentioned in
Section

4.2.1
are still required.
For the sake of simplicity we do not analyze them step by step as the motivating examples can be
straightforwardly adapted. Instead, we directly show how to enforce them.

Remark 5.0.1
.

The need of the type system that we present in this chapter comes from the fact the there
exist multiparty sessions that cannot be decomposed in binary ones.
Consider the following local types

\begin{array}[]{lcl}S_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}}&:&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{!}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}c}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end},\,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}b}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\}\\
S_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}}&:&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ok}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end},\,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}b}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}no}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\}\\
S_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}}&:&\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ok}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}c}.{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end},\,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}no}.{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\}\end{array}

This example is paradigmatic because of the dependencies in the communications.
Indeed,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}
receives
c
from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
only if
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}
receives
a
from
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
.
Furthermore, the class of multiparty sessions that can be decomposed into linear
ones consists of those sessions whose local types can be
projected
to all the participants
appearing in them.
(
i.e.
the
projections
exist).
This is not true for the example above because the projections of
S_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}}
on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}
and
of
S_{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}}
on
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
are not defined as is such types the communication with
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}

does not take place in all the branches.

\lrcorner

The chapter is organized as follows.
In
Section

5.1
we present the syntax and the semantics of the calculus based on multiparty sessions.

Section

5.2
shows the typing rules for such calculus. Differently from
Section

4.2
, we focus
on some involved examples of processes instead of analyzing additional required properties (see
Section

4.2.1
).
Then, in
Section

5.3
we detail the soundness proof of the type system.
At last, in
Section

5.4
we compare the present type system, and consequently
the one in
Chapter

6
, to existing works.

5.1
Calculus

In this section we define the calculus for multiparty sessions on which we apply
our static analysis technique. The calculus is an extension of the one presented
by
Ciccone and Padovani 2022c
to multiparty sessions in the
style of
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
and that has been presented in
Ciccone et al. 2022
.
We recall some basic notions.
We use an infinite set of
variables
ranged over by
x
,
y
,
z
, an
infinite set of
session names
ranged over by
s
and
t
, a set of

roles
ranged over by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}
,
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}r}
, a set of
message
tags
ranged over by
m
, and a set of
process names
ranged over by

A
,
B
,
C
. As in the binary case, the different terminology for labels
is needed to avoid confusion with the labels in
Section

1.3
.
We use roles to distinguish the participants of a session. In particular, an

endpoint

s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]
consists of a session name
s
and a role

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
and is used by the participant with role
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
to interact with the
other participants of the session
s
.
We use
u
and
v
to range over
channels
, which are either variables or
session endpoints.
We write
\overline{x}
and
\overline{u}
to denote possibly empty sequences of
variables and channels, extending this notation to other entities.
We use
\pi
to range over the elements of the set
\{\mathsf{?},\mathsf{!}\}
of

polarities
, distinguishing input actions (
\mathsf{?}
) from output actions
(
\mathsf{!}
).

5.1.1
Syntax of Processes

\begin{array}[t]{@{}rcll@{}}P,Q,R&::=&&\textbf{Process}\\
&&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}&\text{termination}\\
&|&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,u.{P}&\text{signal in}\\
&|&u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{?}(x).P&\text{channel in}\\
&|&u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}\}_{i\in I}&\text{tag in/out}\\
&|&(s)(P_{1}\mathbin{\mid}\cdots\mathbin{\mid}P_{n})&\text{session}\\
\end{array}~\begin{array}[t]{@{}rcll@{}}\\
&|&A\langle\overline{u}\rangle&\text{invocation}\\
&|&\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,u&\text{signal out}\\
&|&u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{!}v.P&\text{channel out}\\
&|&P\oplus Q&\text{choice}\\
&|&\lceil u\rceil P&\text{cast}\\
\end{array}

Figure 5.1:
Syntax of processes

A
program
is a finite set of
definitions
of the form

A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P
, at most one for each process name, where
P
is a
term generated by the syntax shown in
Figure

5.1
.
The term
done
denotes the terminated process that performs no action.
The term
A\langle\overline{u}\rangle
denotes the invocation of the process with
name
A
passing the channels
\overline{u}
as arguments. When

\overline{u}
is empty we just write
A
instead of
A\langle\rangle
.
The term
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,u
denotes the process that sends a termination signal on
the channel
u
, whereas
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,u.P
denotes the process that waits
for a termination signal from channel
u
and then continues as
P
.
The term
u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{!}v.P
denotes the process that sends the channel

v
on the channel
u
to the role
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
and then continues as

P
. Dually,
u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{?}(x).P
denotes the process that receives a
channel from the role
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
on the channel
u
and then continues as
P

where
x
is replaced with the received channel.
The term
u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
denotes a process that
exchanges one of the tags
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}
on the channel
u
with the role
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}

and then continues as
P_{i}
. Whether the tag is sent or received depends on the
polarity
\pi
and, as it will be clear from the operational semantics, the
polarity
\pi
also determines whether the process behaves as an internal
choice (when
\pi
is
\mathsf{!}
) or an external choice (when
\pi
is
\mathsf{?}
).
In the first case the process chooses
actively
the tag being sent,
whereas in the second case the process reacts
passively
to the tag being
received.
We assume that
I
is finite and non-empty and also that the tags
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}
are
pairwise distinct. For brevity, we write
u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.P_{k}

instead of
u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
when
I
is the
singleton set
\{k\}
.
The term
P\oplus Q
denotes a process that non-deterministically behaves
either as
P
or as
Q
.

A term
(s)(P_{1}\mathbin{\mid}\cdots\mathbin{\mid}P_{n})
with
n\geq 1
denotes the parallel
composition of
n
processes, each of them being a participant of the session

s
. Each process is associated with a distinct a role
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{i}
and
communicates in
s
through the endpoint
s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{i}]
. Combining session
creation and parallel composition in a single form is common in session type
systems based on linear
logic
[
Caires et al. 2016
,
Wadler 2014
,
Lindley and Morris 2016
]
and helps
guaranteeing deadlock freedom.
Finally, a
cast

\lceil u\rceil P
denotes a process that behaves exactly
as
P
. This form is only relevant for the type system and
denotes the fact that the type of
u
is subject to an application of
subtyping.

The free and bound names of a process are defined as usual, the latter ones
being easily recognizable as they occur within round parenteses. We write

\mathsf{fn}(P)
for the set of free names of
P
and we identify processes modulo
renaming of bound names. Note that
\mathsf{fn}(P)
may contain variables and session
names, but not endpoints.
Occasionally we write
A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P
as a predicate or side condition,
meaning that
P
is the process associated with the process name
A
. For each
of such definitions we assume that
\mathsf{fn}(P)\subseteq\{\overline{x}\}
.

5.1.2
Operational Semantics

\begin{array}[]{@{}lr@{~}c@{~}ll@{}}\hypertarget{rule:sm-par-comm}{\textsc{\small[sm-par-comm]}}&(s)(\overline{P}\mathbin{\mid}P\mathbin{\mid}Q\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})&\preccurlyeq&(s)(\overline{P}\mathbin{\mid}Q\mathbin{\mid}P\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})\\
\hypertarget{rule:sm-par-assoc}{\textsc{\small[sm-par-assoc]}}&(s)(\overline{P}\mathbin{\mid}(t)(R\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q}))&\preccurlyeq&(t)((s)(\overline{P}\mathbin{\mid}R)\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})&\text{if $s\in\mathsf{fn}(R)$}\\
&&&&\text{and $t\not\in\mathsf{fn}(P)$,}\\
&&&&\text{$\forall Q.s\not\in\mathsf{fn}(Q)$}\\
\hypertarget{rule:sm-cast-comm}{\textsc{\small[sm-cast-comm]}}&\lceil u\rceil{\lceil v\rceil{P}}&\preccurlyeq&\lceil v\rceil{\lceil u\rceil{P}}\\
\hypertarget{rule:sm-cast-new}{\textsc{\small[sm-cast-new]}}&(s)(\lceil s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\rceil P\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})&\preccurlyeq&(s)(P\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})\\
\hypertarget{rule:sm-cast-swap}{\textsc{\small[sm-cast-swap]}}&(s)(\lceil t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\rceil{P}\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})&\preccurlyeq&\lceil t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\rceil{(s)(P\mathbin{\mid}\overline{Q})}&\text{if $s\neq t$}\\
\hypertarget{rule:sm-call}{\textsc{\small[sm-call]}}&A\langle\overline{u}\rangle&\preccurlyeq&P\{\overline{u}/\overline{x}\}&\text{if $A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P$}\end{array}

Figure 5.2:
Structural precongruence of processes

\frac{}{P_{1}\oplus P_{2}\to P_{k}}\quad\textsc{rm-choice}~k\in\{1,2\}\hypertarget{rule:rm-choice}{}\and\frac{}{(s)(\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}].{P}\mathbin{|}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}_{1}]\mathbin{|}\cdots\mathbin{|}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}_{n}])\to P}\quad\textsc{rm-signal}\hypertarget{rule:rm-signal}{}\and\frac{}{(s)(s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\mathsf{!}v.P\mathbin{|}s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{?}(x).Q\mathbin{|}\overline{R})\to(s)(P\mathbin{|}Q\{v/x\}\mathbin{|}\overline{R})}\quad\textsc{rm-channel}\hypertarget{rule:rm-channel}{}\and\frac{}{(s)(s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\mathsf{!}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{|}\overline{Q})\to(s)(s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.P_{k}\mathbin{|}\overline{Q})}\quad\textsc{rm-pick}~k\in I\hypertarget{rule:rm-pick}{}\and\frac{}{(s)(s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}.P\mathbin{|}s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.Q_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathbin{|}\overline{R})\to(s)(P\mathbin{|}Q_{k}\mathbin{|}\overline{R})}\quad\textsc{rm-tag}~k\in I\hypertarget{rule:rm-tag}{}\and\frac{P\to Q}{(s)(P\mathbin{|}\overline{R})\to(s)(Q\mathbin{|}\overline{R})}\quad\textsc{rm-par}\hypertarget{rule:rm-par}{}\and\frac{P\to Q}{\lceil u\rceil{P}\to\lceil u\rceil{Q}}\quad\textsc{rm-cast}\hypertarget{rule:rm-cast}{}\and\frac{P\preccurlyeq P^{\prime}\quad P^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime}\quad Q^{\prime}\preccurlyeq Q}{P\to Q}\quad\textsc{rm-struct}\hypertarget{rule:rm-struct}{}

Figure 5.3:
Reduction of processes

The operational semantics of processes is given by the structural precongruence
relation
\preccurlyeq
defined in
Figure

5.2
and the reduction relation
\to

defined in
Figure

5.3
. As usual, structural precongruence allows us to
rearrange the structure of processes without altering their meaning, whereas
reduction expresses an actual computation or interaction step.
The adoption of a structural
pre
congruence (as opposed to a more common
congruence relation) is not strictly necessary, but it simplifies the technical
development by reducing the number of cases we have to consider in proofs
without affecting the properties of the calculus in any way (see
Remark

4.1.1
).

Rules
[sm-par-comm]
and
[sm-par-assoc]
state commutativity and
associativity of parallel composition of processes (we write
\overline{P}
to
denote possibly empty parallel compositions of processes). In

[sm-par-assoc]
, the side condition
s\in\mathsf{fn}(R)
makes sure that
R
is
indeed a participant of the session
s
.
We write
\forall Q.s\not\in\mathsf{fn}(Q)

to state that
s
is not free in each of the processes
\overline{Q}
.
Moreover, note that this rule only states
right-to-left associativity. Left-to-right associativity is derivable from this
rule and repeated uses of
[sm-par-comm]
.
Rule
[sm-cast-comm]
allows us to swap two consecutive casts.
Rule
[sm-cast-new]
removes an unguarded cast on an endpoint of the
restricted session (we refer to this operation as “performing the cast”).
Rule
[sm-cast-swap]
swaps a cast and a restricted session as long as the
endpoint in the cast refers to a different session.
Finally, rule
[sm-call]
unfolds a process invocation to its definition.
Hereafter, we write
\{u/x\}
for the capture-avoiding substitution of each
free occurrence of
x
with
u
and
\{\overline{u}/\overline{x}\}
for its
natural extension to equal-length tuples of variables and names.
The rules
[sm-cast-new]
,
[sm-cast-swap]
and
[sm-call]
are
not invertible: by
[sm-cast-new]
casts can only be removed but never
added; by
[sm-cast-swap]
casts can only be moved closer to their
restriction, so that they can be eventually performed by
[sm-cast-new]
;
by
[sm-call]
process invocations can only be unfolded.

The reduction relation is quite standard.
Rule
[rm-choice]
reduces
P_{1}\oplus P_{2}
to either
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
, non
deterministically.
Rule
[rm-signal]
terminates a session in which all participants
(
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}_{n}
) but one (
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
) are sending a termination signal
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
is waiting for it; the resulting process is the continuation of the
participant
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
.
Rule
[rm-channel]
models the exchange of a channel among two participants
of a session.
Rule
[rm-pick]
models an internal choice whereby a process picks one
particular tag
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{k}
to send on a session.
Rule
[rm-tag]
synchronizes two participants
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}
on
the tag chosen by
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}
.
Finally, rules
[rm-par]
,
[rm-cast]
and
[rm-struct]
close
reductions under parallel compositions and casts and by structural precongruence.

5.1.3
Examples

In the rest of this section we illustrate the main features of the calculus with
some examples. For none of them the existing multiparty session type systems are
able to guarantee progress.
First of all, we formally define in our calculus the processes corresponding to
(a slightly different)
buyer
,
seller
and
carrier
from
Example

2.1.1
that was only informally
presented.

Example 5.1.1

(Buyer - Seller - Carrier)
.

Consider the following definitions:

Main

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}(s)(\textit{Buyer}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}buyer}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Seller}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}seller}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Carrier}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}carrier}]\rangle)

\displaystyle\textit{Buyer}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}seller}]\mathsf{!}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}seller}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}

\displaystyle\textit{Seller}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}buyer}]\mathsf{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.\textit{Seller}\langle x\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}carrier}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}

\displaystyle\textit{Carrier}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}seller}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}

Note that the buyer either sends
pay
or it sends two
add
messages in a
row before repeating this behavior. That is, this particular buyer always adds
an even number of items to the shopping cart.
Nonetheless, the buyer periodically has a chance to send a
pay
message and
terminate. Therefore, the execution of the program in which the buyer only
sends
add
is unfair according to
Definition

2.2.3
hence this program is
fairly terminating.

\lrcorner

Example 5.1.2

(Purchase with negotiation)
.

Consider a variation of
Example

2.1.1
in which the buyer, before making the
payment, negotiates with a secondary buyer for an arbitrarily long time. The
interaction happens in two nested sessions, an outer one involving the primary
buyer, the seller and the carrier, and an inner one involving only the two
buyers. We model the interaction as the program below, in which we collapse
role names to their initials.

Main

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}(s)(\textit{Buyer}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Seller}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Carrier}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}]\rangle)

\displaystyle\textit{Buyer}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}query}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}price}.(t)(\textit{Buyer}_{1}\langle x,t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{1}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Buyer}_{2}\langle t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}]\rangle)

\displaystyle\textit{Seller}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}query}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}price}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\mathsf{?}\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x,\\
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}cancel}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}cancel}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}\end{array}

\displaystyle\textit{Carrier}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ship}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}box}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}cancel}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}

\displaystyle\textit{Buyer}_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}]\mathsf{!}\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}split}.y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}]\mathsf{?}\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}yes}.\lceil x\rceil x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ok}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}box}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done},\\
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}no}.\textit{Buyer}_{1}\langle x,y\rangle\},\end{array}\\
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}giveup}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.\lceil x\rceil x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}cancel}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}\}\end{array}

\displaystyle\textit{Buyer}_{2}(y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{1}]\mathsf{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}split}.y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{1}]\mathsf{!}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}yes}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,y,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}no}.\textit{Buyer}_{2}\langle y\rangle\},\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}giveup}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,y\}

The buyer queries the seller which replies with a price. At this point,

Buyer
creates a new session
t
and forks as a primary buyer
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
and
a secondary buyer
\textit{Buyer}_{2}
. The interaction between the two sub-buyers goes
on until either
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
gives up or
\textit{Buyer}_{2}
accepts its share of the
price. In the former case, the primary buyer waits for the internal session to
terminate and
cancel
s the order with the seller which, in turn, aborts the
transaction with the carrier. In the latter case, the buyer confirms the order
to the seller, which then instructs the carrier to
ship
a
box
to the
buyer.

Note that the outermost session
s
, taken in isolation, terminates in a
bounded number of interactions, but its progress cannot be established without
assuming that the innermost session
t
terminates. In particular, if the two
buyers keep negotiating forever, the seller and the carrier starve.
However, the innermost session can terminate if
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
sends
giveup
to

\textit{Buyer}_{2}
or if
\textit{Buyer}_{2}
sends
yes
to
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
. Thus, the run in
which the two buyers negotiate forever is unfair, the session
t
fairly
terminates and the session
s
terminates as well.

On the technical side, note that the definition of
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
contains two
casts on the variable
x
. As we will see in
example

5.2.2
, these casts
are necessary for the typeability of
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
to account for the fact that

x
is used
differently
in two distinct branches of the process.

\lrcorner

Example 5.1.3

(Parallel merge sort)
.

To illustrate an example of program that creates an unbounded number of
sessions we model a parallel version of the merge sort algorithm.

Main

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}(s)(s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}req}.s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}res}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,s.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}\mathbin{|}\textit{Sort}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}]\rangle)

\displaystyle\textit{Sort}(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}req}.

\displaystyle((t)(\textit{Merge}\langle x,t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}]\rangle\mathbin{|}\textit{Sort}\langle t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{1}]\rangle\mathbin{|}\textit{Sort}\langle t[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{2}]\rangle)\oplus x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}res}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x)

\displaystyle\textit{Merge}(x,y)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{1}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}req}.y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{2}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}req}.y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{1}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}res}.y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{2}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}res}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}res}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x

The program starts as a single session
s
in which a master
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}m}
sends
the initial collection of data to the worker
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}
as a
req
message
and waits for the
res
ult. The worker is modeled as a process
Sort
that
decides whether to sort the data by itself (right branch of the choice in

Sort
), in which case it sends the
res
ult directly to the master, or to
partition the collection (left branch of the choice in
Sort
). In the latter
case, it creates a new session
t
in which it sends
req
uests to two
sub-workers
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{1}
and
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}w}_{2}
, it gathers the partial
res
ults
from them and gets back to the master with the complete
res
ult.

Since a worker may always choose to start two sub-workers in a new session,
the number of sessions that may be created by this program is unbounded. At
the same time, each worker may also choose to complete its task without
creating new sessions. So, while in principle there exists a run of this
program that keeps creating new sessions forever, this run is unfair according
to
Definition

2.2.3
.

\lrcorner

5.2
Type System

In this section we describe the type system for the calculus of multiparty
sessions of
Section

5.1
.
The typing judgments have the form
\Gamma\vdash^{n}P
, meaning that the process

P
is well typed in the typing context
\Gamma
and has rank
n
. As usual, the

typing context
is a map associating channels with session types (
Section

1.3.2
) and is
meant to contain an association for each name in
\mathsf{fn}(P)
. We write
u_{1}:S_{1},\dots,u_{n}:S_{n}
for the map with domain
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\}
that associates

u_{i}
with
S_{i}
. Occasionally we write
\overline{u:S}
for the same context,
when the number and the specific associations are unimportant. We also assume
that endpoints occurring in a typing context have different session names. That
is,
s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}],s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
implies
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}
.
This constraint makes sure that each well-typed process plays exactly one role
in each of the sessions in which it participates. It is also a common assumption
made in all multiparty session calculi.
We use
\Gamma
and
\Delta
to range over typing contexts, we write
\emptyset

for the empty context and
\Gamma,\Delta
for the union of
\Gamma
and
\Delta
when
they have disjoint domains and disjoint sets of session names.
The
rank

n
in a typing judgment estimates the number of sessions that

P
has to create and the number of casts that
P
has to perform in order to
terminate. The fact that the rank is finite suggests that so is the effort
required by
P
to terminate.

5.2.1
Typing Rules

\frac{\mathstrut}{\emptyset\vdash^{n}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}}\quad\textsc{tm-done}\hypertarget{rule:tm-done}{}\and\frac{\overline{u:S}\vdash^{n}P\{\overline{u}/\overline{x}\}}{\overline{u:S}\vdash^{n+m}A\langle\overline{u}\rangle}\quad\textsc{tm-call}~A:[\overline{S};n],A(\overline{x})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P\hypertarget{rule:tm-call}{}\and\frac{\Gamma\vdash^{n}P}{\Gamma,u:{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\vdash^{n}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,u.{P}}\quad\textsc{tm-wait}\hypertarget{rule:tm-wait}{}\and\frac{\mathstrut}{u:{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\vdash^{n}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,u}\quad\textsc{tm-close}\hypertarget{rule:tm-close}{}\and\frac{\Gamma,u:T,x:S\vdash^{n}P}{\Gamma,u:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{?}{S}.T\vdash^{n}u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{?}(x).P}\quad\textsc{tm-channel-in}\hypertarget{rule:tm-channel-in}{}\and\frac{\Gamma,u:T\vdash^{n}P}{\Gamma,u:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}{!}{S}.T,v:S\vdash^{n}u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{!}v.P}\quad\textsc{tm-channel-out}\hypertarget{rule:tm-channel-out}{}\\
\frac{\forall i\in I:\Gamma,u:S_{i}\vdash^{n}P_{i}}{\textstyle\Gamma,u:\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\vdash^{n}u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\quad\textsc{tm-tag}\hypertarget{rule:tm-tag}{}\and\frac{\Gamma\vdash^{n_{1}}P_{1}\quad\Gamma\vdash^{n_{2}}P_{2}}{\Gamma\vdash^{n_{k}}P_{1}+P_{2}}\quad\textsc{tm-choice}~k\in\{1,2\}\and\frac{\Gamma,u:T\vdash^{n}P}{\Gamma,u:S\vdash^{m+n}\lceil u\rceil{P}}\quad\textsc{tm-cast}~S\leqslant_{m}T\hypertarget{rule:tm-cast}{}\and\frac{\forall i\in\{1,\dots,h\}:\Gamma_{i},s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{i}]:S_{i}\vdash^{n_{i}}P_{i}}{\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{h}\vdash^{1+n_{1}+\cdots+n_{h}}(s)(P_{1}\mathbin{|}\cdots\mathbin{|}P_{h})}\quad\textsc{tm-par}~{\#}\{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}_{i}\triangleright S_{i}\}_{i=1..h}\hypertarget{rule:tm-par}{}\and{\frac{\Gamma,u:S_{k}\vdash^{n}P_{k}}{\textstyle\Gamma,u:\sum_{i\in I}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}\pi\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.S_{i}\vdash^{n}u[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\pi\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}m}_{i}.P_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\quad\textsc{com-tag}}~k\in I\hypertarget{rule:com-tag}{}\and{\frac{\Gamma\vdash^{n}P_{k}}{\Gamma\vdash^{n}P_{1}\oplus P_{2}}\quad\textsc{com-choice}}~k\in\{1,2\}\hypertarget{rule:com-choice}{}

Figure 5.4:
Typing rules

The typing rules are shown in
Figure

5.4
as a
generalized inference
system
(see
Section

1.2
)
in which, we recall, the singly-lined rules are interpreted coinductively
and the called
corules
are interpreted inductively.
For more details about the interpretation, we refer to
Section

1.2
.
We type check a program
\{A_{i}(\overline{x_{i}})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}P_{i}\}_{i\in I}

under a global set of assignments
\{A_{i}:[\overline{S_{i}};n_{i}]\}_{i\in I}

associating each process name
A_{i}
with a tuple of session types
\overline{S_{i}}
,
one for each of the variables in
\overline{x_{i}}
, and a rank
n_{i}
. The program is
well typed if
\overline{x_{i}:S_{i}}\vdash^{n_{i}}P_{i}
is derivable for every
i\in I
, establishing that the tuple
\overline{S_{i}}
corresponds to the way the
variables
\overline{x_{i}}
are used by
P_{i}
and that
n_{i}
is a feasible rank
annotation for
P_{i}
. We now describe the typing rules in detail.

The rule
[tm-done]
states that the terminated process is well typed in
the empty context, to make sure that no unused channels are left behind. Note
that
done
can be given any rank, since it performs no casts and it creates
no new sessions.
The rule
[tm-call]
checks that a process invocation

A\langle\overline{u}\rangle
is well typed by unfolding
A
into the process
associated with
A
. The types associated with
\overline{u}
must match those of
the global assignment
A:[\overline{S};n]
and the rank of the process must
be no greater than that of the invocation. The potential mismatch between the
two ranks improves typeability in some corner cases.
The rules
[tm-wait]
and
[tm-close]
concern processes that exchange
termination signals. The channel being closed is consumed and, in the case of

[tm-wait]
, no longer available in the continuation
P
. Again,

\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,u
can be typed with any rank whereas the rank of
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,u.P

coincides with that of
P
.
The rules
[tm-channel-in]
and
[tm-channel-out]
deal with the
exchange of channels in a quite standard way. As in
Section

4.2.2
,
note that the actual type of the
exchanged channel is required to coincide with the expected one. In particular,
no covariance or contravariance of input and output respectively is allowed.
Relaxing the typing rule in this way would introduce implicit applications of
subtyping that may compromise fair termination (see
Example

4.2.11
). In our
type system, each application of subtyping must be explicitly accounted for as
we will see when discussing
[tm-cast]
.
Rule
[tm-tag]
deals with the exchange of tags. Channels that are not used for such
communication must be used in the same way in all branches, whereas the type of
the channel on which the message is exchanged changes accordingly. All branches
are required to have the same rank, which also corresponds to the rank of the
process. Unlike other presentations of this typing rule
[
Gay and Hole 2005
]
, we
require the branches in the process to be matched exactly by those in the type.
Again, this is to avoid implicit application of subtyping, which might
jeopardize fair termination.
The rule
[tm-choice]
deals with non-deterministic choices and requires
both continuations to be well typed in the same typing context. The judgment in
the conclusion inherits the rank of one of the processes, typically the one with
minimum rank. As we will see in
Example

5.2.5
, this makes it possible to
model finite-rank processes that may create an unbounded number of sessions or
that perform an unbounded number of casts.

The rule
[tm-cast]
models the substitution principle induced by fair
subtyping: when
S\leqslant_{m}T
(
Figure

3.3
), a channel of type
S
can be used where a
channel of type
T
is expected or, in dual fashion
[
Gay 2016
]
, a process
using
u
according to
T
can be used in place of a process using
u
according
to
S
. To keep track of this cast, the rank in the conclusion is augmented by
the weight
m
of the subtyping relation between
S
and
T
.
Note that the typing rule guesses the target type of the cast.

Finally, the rule
[tm-par]
deals with session creation and parallel
composition. This rule is inspired to the
multiparty cut
rule found in
linear logic interpretations of multiparty session
types
[
Carbone et al. 2016
,
Carbone et al. 2017
]
and provides a
straightforward way for enforcing deadlock freedom. Each process in the
composition must be well typed in a slice of the typing context augmented with
the endpoint corresponding to its role. The session map of the new session must
be coherent (
Definition

2.3.2
), implying that it fairly terminates.
The rank of the composition is
one plus the aggregated rank of the composed processes, to account for the fact
that one more session has been created. Recall that coherence is a property
expressed on the LTS of session maps (
Definition

2.3.2
) in line with the
approach of
Scalas and Yoshida 2019
.

The typing rules described so far are interpreted
coinductively
. That is,
in order for a rank
n
process
P
to be well typed in
\Gamma
there must be a

possibly infinite
derivation tree built with these rules and whose
conclusion is the judgment
\Gamma\vdash^{n}P
. But in a generalized inference
system like the one we are defining, this is not enough to establish that
P
is
well typed. In addition, it must be possible to find
finite
derivation
trees for all of the judgments occurring in this possibly infinite derivation
tree using the discussed rules
and possibly
the corules, which we are
about to describe.
Since the additional derivation trees must be finite, all of their branches must
end up with an application of
[tm-done]
or
[tm-close]
, which are
the only axioms in
Figure

5.4
corresponding to the only terminated processes
in
Figure

5.1
. So, the purpose of these finite typing derivations is
to make sure that in every well-typed (sub-)process there exists a path that
leads to termination. On the one hand, this is a sensible condition to require
as our type system is meant to enforce fair process termination. On the other
hand, insisting that these finite derivations can be built using only the typing
rules discusses thus far is overly restrictive, for a process might have

one
path that leads to termination, but also alternative paths that lead
to (recursive) process invocations. In fact, all of the processes we have
discussed in
Examples

5.1.1
,
5.1.2
and
5.1.3
are structured like this. The two
corules
[com-choice]
and
[com-tag]
in
Figure

5.4
establish
that, whenever a multi-branch process is dealt with, it suffices for
one

of the branches to lead to termination. A key detail to note in the case of

[com-choice]
is that the rank of the non-deterministic choice coincides
with that of the branch that leads to termination. This makes sense recalling
that the rank associated with a process represents the overall effort required
for that process to terminate.

Let us recap the notion of well-typed process resulting from the typing rules of

Figure

5.4
.

Definition 5.2.1

(Well-typed process)
.

We say that
P
is
well typed
in the context
\Gamma
and has rank
n
if

1.

There exists an arbitrary (possibly infinite) derivation tree obtained
using the rules in
Figure

5.4
and whose conclusion is

\Gamma\vdash^{n}P

2.

For each judgment in such tree there is a finite
derivation obtained using the rules and the corules

Now we can prove a strong soundness result for our type system, stating that
well-typed, closed processes can always successfully terminate no matter how they reduce.
We analyze the proof in details in
Section

5.3
.

Theorem 5.2.1

(Soundness)
.

If
\emptyset\vdash^{n}P
and
P\Rightarrow Q
, then
Q\Rightarrow\preccurlyeq\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}
.

The implications of
Theorem

4.2.1
that we presented in
Section

4.2

still hold. Notably, the proof schema of
Theorem

5.2.1
follows that of
Theorem

4.2.1
.

5.2.2
Examples

We dedicate the rest of
Section

5.2
to the analysis of some examples
that integrate all the features of the presented type system. We start from some
basic examples and then we move to more involved ones. First, in
Example

5.2.1
we take into
account our slightly different variant of the running example (
Example

5.1.1
).
For what concerns the problematic processes in
Section

4.2.1
, they are still
valid in the multiparty context (see
Remark

5.2.1
).
We use the rest of the examples to deal with the processes introduced in
Section

5.1.3
.

Remark 5.2.1

(Boundedness)
.

All the problematic processes that we presented in
Section

4.2.1
are still valid
in the multiparty scenario and can be dealt with using the techniques that we mentioned for
the binary case. In particular

Action-boundedness.

Type system with corules.

A\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}A\qquad B\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}{B\oplus B}\qquad C\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}{C\oplus\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}}

Session-boundedness.

Rule
[tm-par]
increases the rank by one.

A\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}(s)(s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\mathsf{!}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}],\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}b}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}].{A}\}\mathbin{|}s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}][\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}p}]\mathsf{?}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}a}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}].{A},\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}b}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}q}]\})

Cast-boundedness.

Rule
[tm-cast]
increases the rank by the weight of the subtyping
being applied.

B(x)\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}\lceil x\rceil x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}seller}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.B\langle x\rangle

\lrcorner

Example 5.2.1
.

Let us show some typing derivations for fragments of
Example

5.1.1
.
Let
S_{b}
,
S_{s}
and
S_{c}
be the types from
Example

2.3.2
.
We collapse roles to their initials.
Let
S^{\prime}_{b}=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S^{\prime}_{b}+\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
.
Concerning
Buyer
, we obtain the infinite derivation

\displaystyle\penalty\mathstrut\smash{\vdots}

[tm-call]

\displaystyle x:S^{\prime}_{b}\vdash^{0}\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle

[tm-tag]

\displaystyle x:\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.S^{\prime}_{b}\vdash^{0}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle

[tm-close]

\displaystyle x:{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}\vdash^{0}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x

[tm-tag]

\displaystyle x:S^{\prime}_{b}\vdash^{0}x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\{\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}add}.\textit{Buyer}\langle x\rangle,\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}pay}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}close}\,x\}

and, for each judgment in it, it is easy to find a finite derivation possibly
using
[com-tag]
. Concerning
Main
we obtain

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 47.16515pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\mathstrut\smash{\vdots}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=94.33029pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\hyperlink{rule:tm-call}{\textsc{\small[tm-call]}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]:S^{\prime}_{b}\vdash^{0}\textit{Buyer}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\rangle$}}}}\enskip\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 45.2523pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\smash{\vdots}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=90.50461pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]:S_{s}\vdash^{0}\textit{Seller}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\rangle$}}}}\enskip\penalty\vdots

[tm-par]

\displaystyle\emptyset\vdash^{1}(s)(\textit{Buyer}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Seller}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\rangle\mathbin{\mid}\textit{Carrier}\langle s[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}]\rangle)

where the application of
[tm-par]
is justified by the fact that

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}\triangleright{S^{\prime}_{b}}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}\triangleright{S_{s}}\mathbin{|}\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}\triangleright{S_{c}}
is coherent.
We recall that
S_{b}\leqslant_{1}S^{\prime}_{b}
(
Example

3.2.1
).
No participant creates new sessions or performs casts, so they all have zero
rank. The rank of
Main
is 1 since it creates the session
s
.

\lrcorner

Example 5.2.2
.

In this example we show that the process
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
playing the role
\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{1}

in the inner session of
Example

5.1.2
is well typed. For clarity, we recall its
definition here:

Buyer_{1}(x,y)\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\vartriangle}}{{=}}}}y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}]\mathsf{!}\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}split}.y[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}]\mathsf{?}\{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}yes}.\lceil x\rceil x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ok}.x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}]\mathsf{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}box}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done},\\
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}no}.Buyer_{1}\langle x,y\rangle\},\end{array}\\
\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}giveup}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,y.\lceil x\rceil x[\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}]\mathsf{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}cancel}.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}wait}\,x.\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}done}\}\end{array}

We wish to build a typing derivation showing that
Buyer_{1}
has rank
1
and
uses
x
and
y
respectively according to
S
and
T
, where
S=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}ok}.\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}c}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}box}.{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}+\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}s}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}cancel}.{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}

and
T=\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}split}.(\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}yes}.{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}+\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}no}.T)+\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}{!}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}giveup}.{?}\textsf{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}end}
.
As it has been noted previously, what makes this process interesting is that it
uses the endpoint
x
differently depending on the messages it exchanges with

\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}b}_{2}
on
y
. Since rule
[tm-tag]
requires any endpoint other
than the one on which messages are exchanged to have the same type, the only way

\textit{Buyer}_{2}
can be declared well typed is by means of the casts that occur in its
body.
For the branch in which
\textit{Buyer}_{1}
proposes to
split
the payment we obtain
the following derivation tree (we show only the
yes
branch, the
no
one is trivial):

\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\enskip{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 19.07639pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=38.15277pt\hbox{\kern 3.06668pt$\hyperlink{rule:tm-done}{\textsc{\small[tm-done]}}$}}}\hbox{\kern
